Lusztig’s Jordan decomposition and a finite field instance of relative Langlands duality
Zhicheng Wang

Abstract
. Lusztig [ L5 , L6 ] gave a parametrization for Irr ⁡ ( G F ) \mathrm{Irr}(G^{F}) , where G G is a reductive algebraic group defined over 𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q} , with Frobenius map F F . This parametrization is known as Lusztig’s Jordan decomposition or Lusztig correspondence. However, there is not a canonical choice of Lusztig correspondence. In this paper, we consider classical groups. We pick a canonical choice of Lusztig correspondence which is compatible with parabolic induction and is compatible with theta correspondence. This result extends Pan’s result in [ P3 ] . As an application, we give a refinement of the results of the finite Gan-Gross-Prasad problem in [ Wang1 ] and prove a duality between Theta correspondence and finite Gan-Gross-Prasad problem, which can be regarded as a finite field instance of relative Langlands duality of Ben-Zvi-Sakellaridis-Venkatesh [ BZSV ] .



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 20C33; Secondary 22E50

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Lusztig’s Jordan decomposition

Let
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
be an algebraic closure of a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, which is of characteristic
p>2
. Suppose that the cardinality of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is large
enough so that the main result in
[
Sr1
]
holds. Consider a reductive algebraic group
G
defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, with Frobenius map
F
. Let
\mathcal{E}(G)=\mathrm{Irr}(G^{F})
be the set of irreducible representations of
G^{F}
. We write

\langle\sigma,\pi\rangle_{H^{F}}=\dim\mathrm{Hom}_{H^{F}}(\sigma,\pi),

where
H
is a subgroup of
G
,
\pi\in\mathcal{E}(G)
, and
\sigma\in\mathcal{E}(H)
.
We say
\sigma
occurs in
\pi
or
\sigma\in\pi
if
\langle\sigma,\pi\rangle_{H^{F}}\neq 0
.

In
[
L5
]
, Lusztig provided a way to parametrize
\mathcal{E}(G)
under the assumption that
Z
is a connected component. This parametrization is known as Lusztig’s Jordan decomposition. Later, in
[
L6
]
, this parametrization was extended to cover the case where
Z
is disconnected. With Lusztig’s Jordan decomposition, we obtain a partition of
\mathcal{E}(G)
based on the semisimple conjugacy classes in the dual group
G^{*F}
of
G^{F}
, that is,

(1.1)

\mathcal{E}(G)=\coprod_{(s)}\mathcal{E}(G,s)

where
(s)
runs over
G^{*F}
-conjugacy classes of semisimple elements in the identity component of
G^{*F}
, and
the Lusztig series
\mathcal{E}(G,s)
is defined by

\mathcal{E}(G,s):=\{\pi\in\mathcal{E}(G)|\langle\pi,R_{T^{*},s}^{G}\rangle\neq 0\textrm{ for some }T^{*}\textrm{ containing }s\}.

Here if
G
is a connected reductive group, one has a natural bijection between the set of
G^{F}
-conjugacy classes of the pairs
(T,\theta)
where
T
is an
F
-stable maximal torus of
G
and
\theta
is a character of
T^{F}
, and the set of
G^{*F}
-conjugacy classes of the pairs
(T^{*},s)
where
T^{*}
is an
F
-stable maximal torus of
G^{*}
and contains
s
.
If
(T,\theta)
corresponds to
(T^{*},s)
, we denote by
R_{T^{*},s}^{G}
the Deligne-Lusztig character
R^{G}_{T}(\theta)
of
G^{F}
in
[
DL
]
.
And if
G
is non-connected, we define
R^{G}_{T^{*},s}:=\mathrm{Ind}^{G^{F}}_{(G^{0})^{F}}R^{G^{0}}_{T^{*},s}
.
Lusztig shows that there exists a bijection between the Lusztig series
\mathcal{E}(G,s)
and the unipotent representations
\mathcal{E}(C_{G^{*}}(s),1)
of
C_{G^{*}}(s)^{F}
(see
[
L1
, Chap 9]
),

\mathcal{L}_{s}:\mathcal{E}(G,s)\to\mathcal{E}(C_{G^{*}}(s),1),

extended by linearity to a map between virtual characters satisfying that

(1.2)

\mathcal{L}_{s}(\varepsilon_{G}R^{G}_{T^{*},s})=\varepsilon_{C_{G^{*}}(s)}R^{C_{G^{*}}(s)}_{T^{*},1},

where
\varepsilon_{G}:=(-1)^{\mathrm{rk}\,G}
and
\mathrm{rk}\,G
is the
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-rank of
G
, and
C_{G^{*}}(s)
is the centralizer of
s
in
G^{*}
.
Moreover,
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
sends cuspidal representations to cuspidal representations. Such a bijection
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
will be called a Lusztig correspondence
or Lusztig’s Jordan decomposition.

It should be noted that in general, there is no unique choice of
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
(see
[
GM
, appendix A.5]
). A natural question arises as to whether there is a canonical choice of Lusztig correspondence. In their work
[
DM
]
, Digne and Michel demonstrate that if
G
is a connected group with a connected center, then
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
can be uniquely determined by (
1.2
) and some additional conditions.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}(G)^{\#}
be the subspace of the space of (complex-valued) class functions on
G
spanned by Deligne-Lusztig virtual characters of
G
. Let
\pi^{\#}
be the orthogonal projection of
\pi
onto
{\mathcal{V}}(G)^{\#}
. We say that
\pi
is uniform if
\pi=\pi^{\#}
. Clearly, the image of Lusztig correspondence for uniform representations is uniquely determined by (
1.2
).
If
G
is a general linear group
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
or a unitary group
\mathrm{U}_{n}
, every irreducible representation is uniform. However, if
G
is a symplectic group or an orthogonal group, there exists some ambiguity in Lusztig correspondence. The reason is that
G
or
C_{G^{*}}(s)
might be disconnected.

Let
[\pi]:=\{\pi^{\prime}|(\pi^{\prime})^{\#}=\pi^{\#}\}
be the set of irreducible representations that share the same uniform projection as
\pi
. To differentiate between representations in
[\pi]
, Weil representation and theta lifting are necessary. These have been studied in
[
AMR
,
P1
,
P2
,
MQZ
]
. Based on their results, we know that the images of representations in
[\pi]
under theta correspondence are distinct. In
[
P3
]
, Pan provides an explicit description of
[\pi]
and proves the existence of a unique
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
that satisfies specific compatibility conditions regarding theta correspondence. However, Pan’s choice of
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
depends on the selection of a dual pair of theta correspondence. This means that even orthogonal-symplectic and odd orthogonal-symplectic dual pairs can lead to two different choices of Lusztig correspondence on symplectic groups. Consequently, Pan’s result cannot encompass theta lifting for all dual pairs simultaneously. To describe theta lifting comprehensively, it is necessary to assign names to the irreducible representations or, equivalently, determine a specific choice of Lusztig correspondence. However, once Pan’s
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
is chosen for one dual pair, the theta lifting information for the other dual pair is lost. Therefore, the primary objective of this paper is to establish a canonical choice of
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
that is independent of dual pairs. This
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
will enable the determination of the relationship between theta correspondences for all dual pairs.

1.2.
A finite field instance of relative Langlands duality

For a symmetric space
V_{\mathfrak{n}}
, define its discriminant by

{\mathrm{disc}}(V_{\mathfrak{n}})=(-1)^{\frac{{\mathfrak{n}}({\mathfrak{n}}-1)}{2}}\det(V_{\mathfrak{n}})\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}/\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times 2}.

Write
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}
to be the isometry group of
V_{\mathfrak{n}}
with
{\mathrm{disc}}(V_{\mathfrak{n}})=\epsilon
. For even
{\mathfrak{n}}
, the even orthogonal group
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}
is split if and only if
\epsilon=+
. For odd
{\mathfrak{n}}
, we have
\mathrm{O}^{+}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}
and
\mathrm{O}^{-}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}
are the same as abstract groups; however, they act on two symmetric spaces with different discriminants.

To relate two theta correspondence of dual pairs
(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n^{\prime}})
and
(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}_{2n^{\prime}+1})
, it is natural to consider the restriction of representations from
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}_{2n^{\prime}+1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
to
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
.
Moreover, the Gan-Gross-Prasad problem, which can be seen as a generalization of the above restriction problem, should also be involved.

In the case of local fields, Gan-Gross-Prasad problem
[
GP1
,
GP2
,
GGP1
,
GGP2
]
is related to theta correspondence in the sense of relative Langlands duality of Ben-Zvi-Sakellaridis-Venkatesh
[
BZSV
]
. Let us now recall the relevant context and background as laid out
in
[
BZSV
]
. The relative Langlands program aims to study the relationship between certain periods of automorphic forms on a reductive group
G
and certain special values of automorphic L-functions attached to its dual group
G^{*}
. Despite previous knowledge of some instances of this relationship, they were not well understood in a systematic way. However, there has been recent progress in elucidating these relationships
[
SV
,
BZSV
]
, employing the framework of spherical varieties. This can be seen as an arithmetic analogue of the electric-magnetic duality of boundary conditions in four-dimensional supersymmetric Yang-Mills theory. One noteworthy example of this duality is the duality between the Gan-Gross-Prasad problem and theta correspondence. This specific instance is examined in
[
GW1
,
GW2
]
.

In the case of finite fields, a duality between Gan-Gross-Prasad problem and theta correspondence for unipotent representation has been proven in
[
LW2
,
Wang1
]
. To be more precise, the multiplicity in Gan-Gross-Prasad problem for unipotent representations can be visualized as a diagram:

\textstyle{\pi\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Theta lifting
\scriptstyle{\cal{L}_{1}\circ\cal{AC}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\pi^{\prime},\ \pi^{\prime}\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2m},1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cal{AC}\circ\cal{R}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\star}\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{SO}_{2n+1},1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
unipotent part of GGP
\textstyle{\bigoplus\pi^{\prime\star},\ \pi^{\prime\star}\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{SO}^{\epsilon}_{2m},1),}

where
\cal{L}_{1}
is the Lusztig correspondence and
\cal{R}
is the restriction form
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2m}
to
\mathrm{SO}^{\epsilon}_{2m}
, and
\cal{AC}
is the Alvis-Curtis duality. In this paper, we generalize above commutative diagram to all representations under the canonical choice of Lusztig correspondence.

1.3.
Main results

We recall the description of
C_{G^{*}}(s)
and
\mathcal{E}(C_{G^{*}}(s),1)
(cf. section
3
). We have a decomposition of
C_{G^{*}}(s)
via eigenvalues of
s
as follows:

(1.3)

\displaystyle C_{G^{*}}(s)\cong

\displaystyle G^{*}_{[\neq\pm 1]}(s)\times G^{*}_{[1]}(s)\times G^{*}_{[-1]}(s)\times\begin{cases}\{\pm\},&\textrm{if }G=\mathrm{O}_{2n+1};\\
1,&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}

where
G^{*}_{[\neq\pm 1]}(s)
(resp.
G^{*}_{[1]}(s)
,
G^{*}_{[-1]}(s)
) is the part of
C_{G^{*}}(s)
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\neq 1
(resp.
1
,
-1
) of
s
(cf. section
3
).
In particular, we have a explicit description of
G^{*}_{[a]}(s)
as follows:

•

The group
G^{*}_{[\neq\pm 1]}(s)
is a product of general linear groups and unitary groups;

•

If
G
is a odd orthogonal group, then
G^{*}_{[1]}(s)
and
G^{*}_{[-1]}(s)
are symplectic groups;

•

If
G
is a symplectic group, then
G^{*}_{[1]}(s)
is a special odd orthogonal group and
G^{*}_{[-1]}(s)
is a even orthogonal group;

•

If
G
is a even orthogonal group, then
G^{*}_{[1]}(s)
and
G^{*}_{[-1]}(s)
are even orthogonal groups.

Recall that Lusztig established a bijection between the unipotent representations of orthogonal groups and symplectic groups to equivalence classes of Lusztig’s symbols (see section
2
). Subsequently, choosing
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
leads to a bijection

\pi\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)\to(\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},\iota)

where
\rho
is the projection of
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}(\pi)
on
G^{*}_{[\neq\pm 1]}
, and
\Lambda_{\pm 1}
are the symbol corresponding to the projection of
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}(\pi)
on
G^{*}_{[\pm 1]}
, and
\iota\in\{\pm\}\cong\{\bf{1},\mathrm{sgn}\}
only occur for
G_{n}=\mathrm{O}_{2n+1}
. We denote
\pi
by
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},\iota}
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Suppose that the cardinality of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is large
enough so that the main result in
[
Sr1
]
holds.
Then, for each symplectic group and orthogonal group, there is a unique choice of Lusztig correspondence denoted by
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
. This choice is compatible with parabolic induction and the theta correspondence (cf. Theorem
6.1
).

To outline the strategy of constructing a canonical choice of
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
on
\pi\in\mathcal{E}(G)
, we start by considering an irreducible representation
\pi^{\prime}
occurring in the first descent of
\pi
. This representation is of a group that is smaller than
G
. Here the first descent means that

•

the multiplicity
m_{\epsilon}(\pi,\pi^{\star})\neq 0
(resp.
m_{\epsilon}(\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\star\prime})\neq 0
);

•

the multiplicity
m_{\pm}(\pi,\sigma)\equiv 0
(resp.
m_{\pm}(\pi^{\prime},\sigma)
)) if
\sigma
lives in a group smaller than the group of
\pi^{\star}
(resp.
\pi^{\star\prime}
),

where
m_{\epsilon}(\pi,\pi^{\star})
is the multiplicity in finite Gan-Gross-Prasad problem defined in section
5.1
and
5.2
.
By induction on the rank of
G
, we have the existence of the canonical Lusztig correspondence
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
on
\pi^{\prime}
. We define
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
on
\pi
to be the unique Lusztig correspondence that is compatible with
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
on
\pi^{\prime}
. Since
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
on
\pi^{\prime}
is compatible with theta correspondence and the first descent
\pi^{\prime}
of
\pi
has similar behavior with
\pi
in theta correspondence, the Lusztig correspondence
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
on
\pi
naturally becomes compatible with the theta correspondence (cf. section
6.2
).

Under the canonical choice of Lusztig correspondence in Theorem
1.1
, we distinguish representations in representations in
[\pi]
as follows.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
G
be a symplectic group or orthogonal group. Let
\chi_{G}
be the spinor norm of
G
(cf.
[
Wal2
, 1.6]
). Under the canonical choice of Lusztig correspondence in Theorem
1.1
, we have

\begin{array}[]{c|cccc}G&\pi&\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi&\pi^{c}&\chi_{G}\cdot\pi\\
\hline\cr\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}&\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}}&&\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}^{t}}&\\
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n}&\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}}&\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1}^{t},\Lambda_{-1}^{t}}&\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}^{t}}&\pi_{\rho^{-},\epsilon(\Lambda_{\pm 1})\Lambda_{-1},\epsilon(\Lambda_{\pm 1})\Lambda_{1}}\\
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n+1}&\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},\iota}&\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},-\iota}&&\pi_{\rho^{-},\Lambda_{-1},\Lambda_{1},\iota^{\prime}\iota}\\
\end{array}

where
\Lambda^{t}
is defined in section
2.1
, and
\rho^{-}
is defined in (
3.4
),
\iota^{\prime}=\chi_{G}(-I)
and
\epsilon(\Lambda_{\pm 1}):=(-1)^{\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{1})+\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{-1})}
.

Theorem
1.1
has an important application in the finite Gan-Gross-Prasad problem. In
[
Wang1
,
Wang2
]
, the calculation of the finite Gan-Gross-Prasad problem has been provided. However, the calculation in
[
Wang1
]
depends on how representations behave in theta correspondence. By Theorem
1.1
or Theorem
6.1
, the behavior of representations
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}}
or
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},\iota}
in theta correspondence can be determined based on their subscripts. Hence we can calculate the multiplicity in the finite Gan-Gross-Prasad problem using these subscripts and provides an explicit description of the finite Gan-Gross-Prasad problem in a combinatorial way (Theorem
7.2
).

Combining Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
7.2
, we establish the commutativity between the theta correspondence and the multiplicity in the Gan-Gross-Prasad problem. This commutativity can be regarded as a finite field instance of the relative Langlands duality of Ben-Zvi-Sakellaridis-Venkatesh
[
BZSV
]
. To elaborate, we can visualize the multiplicity in the Gan-Gross-Prasad problem in the following manner:

Theorem 1.3
.

(1)

Let
\pi\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n})
, and
\pi^{\star}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n^{\star}})
.
Then we have the following commutative diagram up to a twist of the sgn characters on orthogonal groups:

\textstyle{\pi\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
GGP
\scriptstyle{\cal{AC}\circ{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\pi^{\star},\ \pi^{\star}\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n^{\star}},s)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cal{AC}\circ{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
theta lfiting
\textstyle{\bigoplus\pi^{\star},\ \rho^{\star}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}}

where the sum in the bottom right corner runs over representations such that

•

\rho=\prod_{[a],a\neq\pm 1}\pi[a]
and
\rho^{\star}=\prod_{[a],a\neq\pm 1}\pi^{\star}[a]
(cf. section
3
);

•

For each
[a]
,
\pi^{\star}[-a]
appears in theta lifting of
\pi[a]
;

•

{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}(\pi^{\star}_{\Lambda_{1}})
appears in theta lifting of
\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}
;

•

\pi^{\star}_{\Lambda_{-1}}
appears in theta lifting of
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}(\pi_{\Lambda_{1}})
.

(2)

Let
\pi\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n})
, and
\pi^{\star}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon^{\star}}_{2n^{\star}+1})
.
Then we have the following commutative diagram up to a twist of the sgn character on orthogonal groups:

\textstyle{\pi\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
GGP
\scriptstyle{\cal{AC}\circ{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\pi^{\star},\ \pi^{\star}\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon^{\star}}_{2n^{\star}},s)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cal{AC}\circ{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
theta lfiting
\textstyle{\bigoplus\pi^{\star},\ \rho^{\star}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}\otimes\iota^{\star}}

where the sum in the bottom right corner runs over representations such that

•

\rho=\prod_{[a],a\neq\pm 1}\pi[a]
and
\rho^{\star}=\prod_{[a],a\neq\pm 1}\pi^{\star}[a]

•

For each
[a]
,
\pi^{\star}[a]
appears in theta lifting of
\pi[a]
;

•

{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}(\pi^{\star}_{\Lambda_{1}})
appears in theta lifting of
\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}
;

•

{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}(\pi^{\star}_{\Lambda_{-1}})
appears in theta lifting of
\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}
.

Remark 1.4
.

The sgn characters for unipotent representations are explicitly given in Theorem
7.3
. For the general cases, the sign characters can be calculated using Theorem
7.2
. However, this process for the general cases can be extremely complicated, as the variables
\rho
,
\Lambda_{1}
, and
\Lambda_{-1}
can all influence each other.

Although the description of the multiplicity in finite Gan-Gross-Prasad problem in Theorem
7.2
is quite complicated, the first descent case of Gan-Gross-Prasad problem, which is similar to the first occurrence case of theta lifting, is simple.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
\pi\in\mathcal{E}(G_{n})
where
G_{n}
is either a symplectic group, odd orthogonal group or a even orthogonal group. Then we have the following commutative diagram

(1.4)

24.54211pt\raise-74.3204pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5853pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{can}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 59.42822pt\raise-71.9801pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 80.90785pt\raise-89.1035pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00002pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Theta_{m,m^{\prime}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 162.13548pt\raise-95.88684pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 76.44907pt\raise-95.88684pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 132.13548pt\raise-95.88684pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 162.13548pt\raise-95.88684pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\pi^{\star\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\ell,\epsilon}(\pi^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 218.6801pt\raise-74.3204pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5853pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{can}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 254.51581pt\raise-71.9801pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 272.76387pt\raise-95.88684pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where all notations are defined in section
7
.

Let us explain the diagram (
1.4
) roughly. Here
\pi^{\star}
(resp.
\pi^{\star\prime}
) is a irreducible representation occurs in the first descent of
\pi
(resp.
\pi^{\prime}
). The index
n^{\prime}
in the theta lifting
\Theta_{n,n^{\prime}}
is the first occurrence index of
\pi
. Therefore, Theorem
1.5
gives a duality between the first descent case of finite Gan-Gross-Prasad problem and first occurrence case of theta correspondence.

This paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we recall the classification of unipotent representations
[
L3
,
L4
,
LS
]
. In Section
3
, we recall the theory of Deligne-Lusztig characters and Lusztig correspondence. Then we discuss the ambiguity of Lusztig correspondence. In Section
4
, we review some results on the theta correspondence for finite symplectic groups and finite orthogonal groups. We also compute the first occurrence indexes and conservation relations, which will help to resolve any ambiguity in the Lusztig correspondence.
In Section
5
, we recall the Gan-Gross-Prasad problem, and establish conservation relations for it, which are the dual to conservation relations of the theta correspondence. In Section
6.2
, we have proven Theorem 6, our main result. This theorem provides a choice of Lusztig correspondence that is compatible with parabolic induction and the theta correspondence. In Section
7
, we calculate the multiplicity
in the finite Gan-Gross-Prasad problem and established the duality between the Gan-Gross-Prasad problem and theta correspondence by the Lusztig correspondence described in Theorem
6.1
.

2.
Classification of unipotent representations

In this section we assume that
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is a finite field of odd characteristic.

2.1.
Lusztig’s symbols

In this section, we follow the notation of
[
P1
]
to review Lusztig’s symbol, which is slightly different from that of
[
L1
]
.

Let
\lambda=[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots]
be a partition of
n
and denote
|\lambda|
to the length
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}
.
We often write
\lambda=[\lambda_{1}^{k_{1}},\cdots,\lambda_{l}^{k_{l}}]
where
\lambda_{j}
are distinct nonzero parts and
k_{j}
are their multiplicity.
As is standard, we realize partitions as Young diagrams and denote by
\lambda^{t}=[\lambda^{t}_{1},\lambda^{t}_{2},\cdots]
the transpose of
\lambda
. A partition
\lambda
is called
even
if all multiplicities
k_{i}
are even.

We say that two partitions
\lambda=[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots]
and
\lambda^{\prime}=[\lambda^{\prime}_{1},\lambda^{\prime}_{2},\dots]
are
close
if
|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i}^{\prime}|\leqslant 1
for every
i
.
Write

\lambda\cap\lambda^{\prime}:=[\lambda_{i}]_{\{i|\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{i}^{\prime}\}}

be the partition formed by the common parts of
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
. Following
[
AMR
]
, we say that
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
are
2-transverse
if they are close and
\lambda\cap\lambda^{\prime}
is even.
In particular, if
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
are close and
\lambda\cap\lambda^{\prime}=\varnothing
, then
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
are 2-transverse, and in this case we say that they are
transverse
. For example, let

\lambda=[\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k}]
be a partition of
n
, and let

\lambda_{\star}=[\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{k}]

be the partition of
n-\lambda_{1}
obtained by removing the first row of
\lambda
. Then
\lambda^{t}
and
\lambda_{\star}^{t}
are 2-transverse. Moreover,
\lambda_{\star}^{t}
is the unique partition of
n-\lambda_{1}
such that
\lambda^{t}
and
\lambda_{\star}^{t}
are 2-transverse.

A
symbol
is an array of the form

\Lambda=\begin{pmatrix}A\\
B\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1},a_{2},\cdots,a_{m_{1}}\\
b_{1},b_{2},\cdots,b_{m_{2}}\end{pmatrix}

of two finite sets
A
,
B
of natural numbers (possibly empty) with
a_{i},b_{i}\geqslant 0
,
a_{i}>a_{i+1}
and
b_{i}>b_{i+1}
.
The
rank
and
defect
of a symbol
\Lambda
are defined by

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}}(\Lambda):=\sum_{a_{i}\in A}a_{i}+\sum_{b_{i}\in B}b_{i}-\left\lfloor\left(\frac{\#A+\#B-1}{2}\right)^{2}\right\rfloor,

\displaystyle\textrm{def}(\Lambda):=\#A-\#B.

Note that the definition of
{\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda)
differs from that of
[
L1
, p.133]
.

For a symbol
\Lambda=\binom{A}{B}
, let
\Lambda^{*}
(resp.
\Lambda_{*}
) denote the first row (resp. second row) of
\Lambda
, i.e.
\Lambda^{*}=A
and
\Lambda_{*}=B
,
and let

\Lambda^{t}:=\binom{B}{A}
. Write

\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathrm{sgn}(\mathrm{def}(\Lambda)),&\textrm{ if }\mathrm{def}(\Lambda)\neq 0;\\
+,&\textrm{ if }\Lambda=\binom{1}{0};\\
-,&\textrm{ if }\Lambda=\binom{0}{1};\\
\end{array}\right.\textrm{ and }\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda):=(-1)^{\left\lfloor\frac{|\mathrm{def}(\Lambda)|}{2}\right\rfloor}

to be the sign and the parity of
\mathrm{def}(\Lambda)
, respectively. Here for the case of
\mathrm{def}(\Lambda)=0
, we only care about the basic case
\Lambda=\binom{1}{0}
or
\binom{0}{1}
(cf. Section
3.3
). Clearly, we have

\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda)=-\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda^{t})\textrm{ and }\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda)=\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda^{t}).

2.2.
Bi-partitions

For partitions
\lambda=[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{k}]
and
\mu=[\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\cdots,\mu_{l}]
, we write

\lambda\preccurlyeq\mu\quad\textrm{if }\mu_{i}-1\leqslant\lambda_{i}\leqslant\mu_{i}\textrm{ for each }i.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{2}(n)

denote the set of bi-partitions
\left[{\begin{smallmatrix}\lambda\\
\mu\end{smallmatrix}}\right]
of
n
, where
\lambda
,
\mu
are partitions
such that
|\lambda|+|\mu|=n
. We can associate a bi-partition to each symbol as follows:

\displaystyle\Upsilon:

\displaystyle\Lambda=\begin{pmatrix}A\\
B\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{m_{1}}\\
b_{1},b_{2},\dots,b_{m_{2}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{bmatrix}\lambda\\
\mu\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}a_{1}-(m_{1}-1),a_{2}-(m_{1}-2),\dots,a_{m_{1}-1}-1,a_{m_{1}}\\
b_{1}-(m_{2}-1),b_{2}-(m_{2}-2),\dots,b_{m_{2}-1}-1,b_{m_{2}}\end{bmatrix}.

This gives a bijection

(2.1)

\Upsilon:\mathcal{S}_{n,\beta}\to\begin{cases}\mathcal{P}_{2}(n-(\frac{\beta+1}{2})(\frac{\beta-1}{2})),&\textrm{if }\ \beta\textrm{ is odd};\\
\mathcal{P}_{2}(n-(\frac{\beta}{2})^{2}),&\textrm{if }\ \beta\textrm{ is even},\end{cases}

where
\mathcal{S}_{n,\beta}
denotes the set of symbols of rank
n
and defect
\beta
. Then a symbol
\Lambda
can be characterized by its defect and the bi-partition
\Upsilon(\Lambda)
.

Set

\Upsilon(\Lambda)^{\epsilon}:=\begin{cases}\lambda,&\textrm{ if }\epsilon=+;\\
\mu,&\textrm{ if }\epsilon=-.\end{cases}

To each symbol
\Lambda
, we associate its Alvis-Curtis dual
{}^{{\bf\mathrm{d}}}\Lambda

by requiring that

\textrm{def}({}^{{\bf\mathrm{d}}}\Lambda)=\textrm{def}(\Lambda)\quad\text{ and }\quad\Upsilon({}^{{\bf\mathrm{d}}}\Lambda)^{\epsilon}=\left(\Upsilon(\Lambda)^{-\epsilon}\right)^{t},

where
\epsilon=\pm
.

2.3.
Classification of unipotent representations of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
and
\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})

In
[
LS
]
, Lusztig and Srinivasan have classified the representations of
G^{F}=\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
and
\operatorname{U}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. They have established a unique one-to-one correspondence between the unipotent representations of
G^{F}
and the irreducible representations of the Weyl group
W_{n}
of
G
.
It is well-known that
W_{n}\cong S_{n}
, where
S_{n}
is the symmetry group, and irreducible representations of
S_{n}
are parametrized by partitions of
n
. We denote the representation corresponding to a partition
\lambda
of
n
by
\pi_{\lambda}
. For example,
\pi_{(n)}
corresponds to the trivial representation of
G^{F}
. By Lusztig’s result
[
L1
]
,
\pi_{\lambda}
is a unipotent cuspidal representation of
\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
if and only if
n=\frac{k(k+1)}{2}
for some positive integer
k
and
\lambda=[k,k-1,\cdots,1]
.

2.4.
Classification of unipotent representations of symplectic groups and orthogonal groups

We now recall the classification of irreducible unipotent representations of symplectic and orthogonal groups. Let
\cal{E}(G_{n},1)
be the collection of unipotent representations of
G_{n}^{F}=\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
\mathrm{SO}_{2n+1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
\mathrm{O}_{2n+1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
and
\mathrm{O}^{\pm}_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Lusztig gives a bijection between the unipotent representations of these groups to equivalence classes of symbols. Then we have the following bijections:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\cal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},1)\\
&\cal{E}(\mathrm{SO}_{2n+1},1)\\
&\cal{E}(\mathrm{O}_{2n+1},1)\\
&\cal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{+}_{2n},1)\\
&\cal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{-}_{2n},1)\end{aligned}\right.\longrightarrow\left\{\begin{aligned} &\cal{S}_{n}:=\big\{\Lambda|\mathrm{rank}(\Lambda)=n,\mathrm{def}(\Lambda)=1\ (\textrm{mod }4)\big\};\\
&\cal{S}_{n};\\
&\cal{S}_{n}\times\{\pm\};\\
&\cal{S}^{+}_{n}:=\big\{\Lambda|\mathrm{rank}(\Lambda)=n,\mathrm{def}(\Lambda)=0\ (\textrm{mod }4)\big\};\\
&\cal{S}^{-}_{n}:=\big\{\Lambda|\mathrm{rank}(\Lambda)=n,\mathrm{def}(\Lambda)=2\ (\textrm{mod }4)\big\}.\end{aligned}\right.

We require that the above bijections satisfy
[
P3
, (3.13)]
, which is a reformulation of
[
L3
, theorem 5.8]
and
[
L4
, theorem 5.13]
. Let
\pi_{\Lambda}
and
\pi_{\Lambda,\iota}
be the irreducible representations parametrized by
\Lambda
and
(\Lambda,\iota)
, respectively.
For symplectic and special odd orthogonal groups, the bijection is uniquely determined by
[
P3
, (3.13)]
. For even orthogonal groups, the bijection is characterized by additional conditions mentioned in
[
P3
, section 5.3]
. It is known that
\pi_{\Lambda^{t}}=\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi_{\Lambda}
.
In the case of odd orthogonal groups, we differentiate
\pi_{\Lambda,\pm}
by deciding that
\pi_{\Lambda,\pm}(-1)=\pm\mathrm{Id}
.
Regarding the representation of the trivial symplectic group
\mathrm{Sp}_{0}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, we associated it with the symbol
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
-\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
As for the trivial group
\mathrm{O}^{+}_{0}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, its trivial representation is associated with the symbol
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-\\
-\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Example 2.1
.

(1)

(
[
P3
, Lemma 4.6]
) Let
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}
, and
\pi_{\Lambda}={\bf 1}
, the trivial representation of
G^{F}
. Then

\Lambda=\begin{pmatrix}n\\
-\end{pmatrix}.

(2)

(
[
P3
, Corollary 5.10]
) Let
G=\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n}
, and
\pi_{\Lambda}={\bf 1}
, the trivial representation of
G^{F}
. Then

\Lambda=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\begin{pmatrix}n\\
0\end{pmatrix}&\textrm{ if }\epsilon=+;\\
\\
\begin{pmatrix}-\\
n,0\end{pmatrix}&\textrm{ if }\epsilon=-.\end{array}\right.

(3)

Let
G=\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n+1}
, and
\pi_{\Lambda,\iota}={\bf 1}
, the trivial representation of
G^{F}
. Then

\Lambda=\begin{pmatrix}n\\
-\end{pmatrix}

and
\iota=+.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
\pi_{\Lambda,\iota}\in{\mathcal{E}}(G_{n},\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime},\iota^{\prime}})
(see section
3.3
for the definition of
{\mathcal{E}}(G_{n},\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime},\iota^{\prime}})
), where
\iota,\iota^{\prime}\in\{\pm\}
, and
\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime},\iota^{\prime}}
is a unipotent cuspidal representation of
G_{m}^{F}
with
m\leq n
, and
\iota
and
\iota^{\prime}
only occurs for odd orthogonal groups. Then we have
\iota=\iota^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{def}(\Lambda)=\mathrm{def}(\Lambda^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Lusztig’s bijection
\cal{E}(G_{n},1)\to\cal{S}_{n}
is compatible with the parabolic induction for symplectic and special odd orthogonal groups. This result is discussed in
[
L1
]
. The odd orthogonal case follows from the special odd orthogonal case. For even orthogonal groups, the compatibility with the parabolic induction is one of the additional conditions mentioned in
[
P3
, section 5.3]
.
∎

2.5.
Symbols of unipotent cuspidal representations

In
[
L1
]
, we know that
\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
(resp,
\mathrm{SO}_{2n+1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
) has a unique irreducible unipotent cuspidal representation if and only if
n=k(k+1)
(resp.
n=k(k+1)
,
n=k^{2}
). We regarded the trivial representations of
\mathrm{Sp}_{0}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
and
\mathrm{O}^{+}_{0}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
as unipotent cuspidal representations.

For
\mathrm{Sp}_{2k(k+1)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, the unique unipotent cuspidal representation
\pi_{\Lambda}
is associated to the symbol:

(2.2)

\Lambda=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\begin{pmatrix}2k,2k-1,\cdots,1,0\\
-\end{pmatrix}\textrm{ if }k\textrm{ is even};\\
&\begin{pmatrix}-\\
2k,2k-1,\cdots,1,0\end{pmatrix}\textrm{ if }k\textrm{ is odd};\\
\end{aligned}\right.

of defect
(-1)^{k}(2k+1)
.

For
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2k^{2}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
with
\epsilon=(-1)^{k}
, there are two unipotent cuspidal representations
\pi_{\Lambda}
and
\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi_{\Lambda}=\pi_{\Lambda^{t}}
where

\Lambda=\begin{pmatrix}2k-1,2k-2,\cdots,1,0\\
-\end{pmatrix}.

For
\mathrm{O}_{2k(k+1)+1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, there are two unipotent cuspidal representations
\pi_{\Lambda,+}
and
\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi_{\Lambda,+}=\pi_{\Lambda,-}
where

(2.3)

\Lambda=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\begin{pmatrix}2k,2k-1,\cdots,1,0\\
-\end{pmatrix}\textrm{ if }k\textrm{ is even};\\
&\begin{pmatrix}-\\
2k,2k-1,\cdots,1,0\end{pmatrix}\textrm{ if }k\textrm{ is odd}.\\
\end{aligned}\right.

3.
Lusztig correspondence

In this section we assume that
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is a finite field of odd characteristic.

3.1.
The structure of Lusztig correspondence

To parametrize the irreducible representations of
G^{F}
, we first follow Lusztig’s Jordan decomposition (see
[
Ma
, Section 2.3]
and
[
Gec
, Theorem 5.2]
for instance) to
partition
{\mathrm{Irr}}(G^{F})
according to the semisimpe conjugacy classes in the dual group
G^{*F}
of
G^{F}
, that is,

(3.1)

{\mathrm{Irr}}(G^{F})=\coprod_{(s)\in(G^{*})^{\circ F}_{ss}/{\mathrm{Ad}}(G^{*F})}\mathcal{E}(G,s)

where
(s)
runs over
G^{*F}
-conjugacy classes of semisimple elements in the identity component of
G^{*F}
, and
the Lusztig series
\mathcal{E}(G,s)
is defined by

\mathcal{E}(G,s):=\{\pi\in\textrm{Irr}(G^{F})|\langle\pi,R_{T^{*},s}^{G}\rangle\neq 0\textrm{ for some }T^{*}\textrm{ containing }s\}.

Here if
G
is a connected reductive group, one has a bijection between the set of
G^{F}
-conjugacy classes of the pairs
(T,\theta)
where
T
is an
F
-stable maximal torus of
G
and
\theta
is a character of
T^{F}
, and the set of
G^{*F}
-conjugacy classes of the pairs
(T^{*},s)
where
T^{*}
is an
F
-stable maximal torus of
G^{*}
and contains
s
.
If
(T,\theta)
corresponds to
(T^{*},s)
, we denote by
R_{T^{*},s}^{G}
the Deligne-Lusztig character
R^{G}_{T}(\theta)
of
G^{F}
in
[
DL
]
.
And if
G
is non-connected, we define
R^{G}_{T^{*},s}:={\mathrm{Ind}}^{G^{F}}_{(G^{0})^{F}}R^{G^{0}}_{T^{*},s}
.

Continuing (
3.1
),
we recall the correspondence between the Lusztig series
{\mathcal{E}}(G,s)
and the unipotent representations
{\mathcal{E}}(C_{G^{*}}(s),1)
of
C_{G^{*}}(s)^{F}
in
[
L1
, Chap 9]
,
[
Sr2
, Theorem 8.14]
and
[
Ma
, Lemma 2.7]
,
where
C_{G^{*}}(s)
is the centralizer of
s
in
G^{*}
.

Proposition 3.1

(
[
DM
, Theorem 11.5.1, Proposition 11.5.6]
)
.

Let
G
be a reductive connected group defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.
There is a bijection

\mathcal{L}_{s}:\mathcal{E}(G,s)\to\mathcal{E}(C_{G^{*}}(s),1),

extended by linearity to a map between virtual characters satisfying that

(3.2)

\mathcal{L}_{s}(\varepsilon_{G}R^{G}_{T^{*},s})=\varepsilon_{C_{G^{*}}(s)}R^{C_{G^{*}}(s)}_{T^{*},1},

where
\varepsilon_{G}:=(-1)^{\mathrm{rk}\,G}
and
\mathrm{rk}\,G
is the
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-rank of
G
;
Moreover,
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
sends cuspidal representations to cuspidal representations.

Lusztig’s Jordan decomposition can be extended to even orthogonal groups (see
[
AMR
, Proposition 1.7]
).

Next, we recall the description of
C_{G^{*}}(s)
in
[
AMR
, Section 1.B]
and then refine
{\mathcal{E}}(C_{G^{*}}(s),1)
.
The multi-set of eigenvalues of
s
on the standard representation of
G^{*}
is of the form

(s)=\begin{cases}\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},x_{1}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1},1\},&\textrm{if }G^{*}=\mathrm{SO}_{2n+1};\\
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},x_{1}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}\},&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}

For
a\in\overline{\mathbb{F}_{q}}^{\times}
, define

\nu_{a}(s):=\#\{i|x_{i}\textrm{ or }x_{i}^{-1}=a\}\quad\textrm{and}\quad[a]:=\{a^{{q}^{k}},a^{-{q}^{k}}|k\in\mathbb{Z}\},

where
\#X
denotes the cardinality of a finite set
X
.
Clearly,
\nu(a)
and
[a]
only depend on the orbit of
a
under
\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}
, the group of automorphisms of
\overline{\mathbb{F}_{q}}^{\times}
generated by the Frobenius map
F
and the inversion
a\mapsto a^{-1}
.
The centralizer
C_{G^{*}}(s)
has a natural decomposition

C_{G^{*}}(s)\cong\begin{cases}\prod_{[a]}G^{*}_{[a]}(s)\times\{\pm\},&\textrm{if }G=\mathrm{O}_{2n+1};\\
\prod_{[a]}G^{*}_{[a]}(s),&\textrm{otherwise},\end{cases}

where
[a]
runs over the orbits
\overline{\mathbb{F}_{q}}^{\times}/\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}
,
and
G^{*}_{[a]}(s)
is the restriction of scalars
{\mathrm{Res}}_{{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(a)/{\mathbb{F}_{q}}}
of a classical group defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}(a)
, with
\overline{\mathbb{F}_{q}}
-rank
\frac{1}{2}\#[a]\cdot\nu_{a}(s)
.
Clearly,
G^{*}_{[a]}(s)
is the trivial group if
\nu_{a}(s)=0
.
For convenience, we set

G^{*}_{[\neq\pm 1]}(s):=\prod_{[a],\,a\neq\pm 1}G^{*}_{[a]}(s).

Then we rewrite

(3.3)

\displaystyle C_{G^{*}}(s)\cong

\displaystyle G^{*}_{[\neq\pm 1]}(s)\times G^{*}_{[1]}(s)\times G^{*}_{[-1]}(s)\times\begin{cases}\{\pm\},&\textrm{if }G=\mathrm{O}_{2n+1};\\
1,&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}

In particular, we have the following explicit description of
G^{*}_{[a]}(s)
:

•

If
a\neq\pm 1
, then
G^{*}_{[a]}(s)
is either
{\mathrm{Res}}_{{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(a)/{\mathbb{F}_{q}}}\mathrm{GL}_{\nu_{a}(s)}
or the unitary group
{\mathrm{Res}}_{{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(a)/{\mathbb{F}_{q}}}\mathrm{U}_{\nu_{a}(s)}
;

•

If
G=\mathrm{O}_{2n+1}
, then we may take
G^{*}=\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}\times\{\pm 1\}=\mathrm{Sp}(V^{*})\times\{\pm 1\}
, in which case

G^{*}_{[\pm 1]}(s)=\mathrm{Sp}_{2\nu_{\pm 1}(s)}=\mathrm{Sp}(V^{*}_{s=\pm 1});

•

If
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}
, then we may take
G^{*}=\mathrm{SO}_{2n+1}=\mathrm{SO}(V^{*})
, in which case

G^{*}_{[1]}(s)=\mathrm{SO}_{2\nu_{1}(s)+1}=\mathrm{SO}(V^{*}_{s=1})
and
G^{*}_{[-1]}(s)=\mathrm{O}_{2\nu_{-1}(s)}=\mathrm{O}(V^{*}_{s=-1})
;

•

If
G=\mathrm{O}_{2n}(V)
, then
G^{*}=\mathrm{O}_{2n}(V^{*})
with
V^{*}=V
, and

G^{*}_{[\pm 1]}(s)=\mathrm{O}_{2\nu_{\pm 1}(s)}=\mathrm{O}(V^{*}_{s=\pm 1}).

Here
V^{*}
is the (skew) symmetric space defining the dual group
G^{*}
,
V^{*}_{s=\pm 1}
is the eigenspace of
s
on
V^{*}
corresonding to the eigenvalue
\pm 1
.
We remark that when
G^{*}
is orthogonal,
\mathrm{O}(V^{*}_{s=\pm 1})
depends on the discriminant of
V^{*}_{s=\pm 1}
.

Now, we can plug (
3.3
) into Proposition
3.1

and decompose

\displaystyle\prod_{[a]}\mathcal{E}(G^{*}_{[a]}(s),1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\cal\displaystyle E}(G^{*}_{[\neq\pm 1]}(s)\times G^{*}_{[1]}(s)\times G^{*}_{[-1]}(s),1)\times\begin{cases}\{\pm\},&\textrm{if }G=\mathrm{O}_{2n+1};\\
1,&\textrm{otherwise},\end{cases}

where by abuse of notation we also denote
\{\pm\}
the irreducible representations of
\{\pm\}\subset G^{*}
if
G=\mathrm{O}_{2n+1}
.
We may write the image of
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
accordingly

\mathcal{L}_{s}(\pi)=\pi_{\neq\pm 1}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\otimes\iota

where
\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
only occurs for
G=\mathrm{O}_{2n+1}
and

\pi_{\neq\pm 1}=\prod_{[a],a\neq\pm 1}\pi{[a]}\textrm{ with }\pi_{[a]}\in\mathcal{E}(G^{*}_{[a]}(s),1).

Recall that irreducible unipotent representations of general linear groups and unitary groups are parametrized by partitions. We can associate each
\pi[a]
with a partition
\lambda[a]
. Let
\cal{P}
be the set of partitions, and
\cal{A}:=\{[a]\in\overline{\mathbb{F}_{q}}^{\times}/\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}|a\neq\pm 1\}
. Let

\cal{P}{A}
be the set

\cal{P}{A}=\left\{\begin{matrix}\rho:&\cal{A}&\to&\cal{P}\\
&[a]&\to&\rho[a]\end{matrix}\right\}.

Therefore, we can identify
\pi_{\neq\pm 1}
with a element
\rho\in\cal{P}{A}
, and denote
\pi
by
\pi^{{\mathcal{L}}_{s}}_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},\iota}
or simply
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},\iota}
, when no confusion raise. By abuse of notations, we regard
\rho
as a irreducible representation in
\prod_{[a],a\neq\pm 1}\mathcal{E}(G^{*}_{[a]}(s),1)
, and write

\mathcal{L}_{s}(\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},\iota})=\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\otimes\iota.

Let
\rho\in\cal{P}{A}
. We define
\rho^{-}
to be an element in
\cal{P}{A}
such that

(3.4)

\rho^{-}:[a]\to\rho[-a],

and we define
\rho_{1}
to be an element in
\cal{P}{A}
such that

(3.5)

\rho_{1}:[a]\to\rho_{1}[a]=\rho[a]_{\star},

which means that
\rho_{1}[a]
is the partition obtained by removing the first row of
\rho[a]
.

For a fixed
\rho\in\cal{P}{A}
, there exists a unique irreducible representation
\pi^{\mathrm{Sp}}_{\rho}
of a symplectic group such that
the
\pm 1
part of
\pi^{\mathrm{Sp}}_{\rho}
is trivial and the
\neq\pm 1
part of
\pi^{\mathrm{Sp}}_{\rho}
corresponds to
\rho
. As we will see in Section
3.2
,

\pi^{\mathrm{Sp}}_{\rho}
does not depend on the choice of Lusztig correspondence. Assume that
\pi^{\mathrm{Sp}}_{\rho}\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},s)
.
Write

|\rho|=n,

and

\iota_{\rho}:=\pi^{\mathrm{Sp}}_{\rho}(-I)\in\{\pm\},\textrm{ and }{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho}:=\iota_{\rho}\varepsilon(-1)^{|\rho|}

and

\epsilon_{\rho}:=(-1)^{\#\{[a]\ |\ G^{*}_{[a]}(s)\text{ is unitary}\}},

where
\varepsilon(-1)
is the square class of
-1
in
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}/(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times})^{2}
.

3.2.
The ambiguity of Lusztig correspondence

It’s worth noting that the choice of
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
is usually not unique. However, if
G
is a connected group with a connected center, we can determine
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
uniquely by imposing some extra conditions
[
DM
, Theorem 7.1]
. Additionally, Pan has proved in
[
P3
]
that for a fixed dual pair, there is a unique choice of
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
that is compatible with theta correspondence. But, it’s important to note that Pan’s choice of
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
depends on the dual pair
(G,G^{\prime})
and not the group
G
. Thus, even orthogonal-symplectic dual pairs and odd orthogonal-symplectic dual pairs can give us two different choices of the Lusztig correspondence on symplectic groups.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}(G)^{\#}
be the subspace of the space of (complex-valued) class functions on
G
spanned by Deligne-Lusztig virtual characters of
G
. We define
\pi^{\#}
as the orthogonal projection of
\pi
on
{\mathcal{V}}(G)^{\#}
. We call
\pi
uniform if
\pi=\pi^{\#}
.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
\pi
be an irreducible representation of a symplectic group or an orthogonal group. Let

[\pi]:=\{\pi^{\prime}|(\pi^{\prime})^{\#}=\pi^{\#}\}.

We cannot differentiate between representations in
[\pi]
using (
3.2
). Therefore, we must determine to what extent an irreducible representation
\pi
can be identified by its uniform projection
\pi^{\#}
.
According to (
3.2
), we have the following result.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)
.

(1)

If
\pi
is uniform, then
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}(\pi)
does not depend on the choice of
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
.

(2)

For any two different choice of Lusztig correspondences
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}^{\prime}
, we have
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}(\pi)\in[{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{s}(\pi)]
.

(3)

We have
\pi\in[\pi^{\prime}]
if and only if
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}(\pi)\in[{\mathcal{L}}_{s}(\pi^{\prime})]
.

Assume that
\pi\in{\mathcal{E}}(G,s)
, and

\mathcal{L}_{s}(\pi)=\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\otimes\iota,

where
\iota\in\{\pm\}
only occurs for
G=\mathrm{O}_{2n+1}
. Note that
\rho
is uniform, so the
\rho
part does not depends on the choice of
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
. For the
\Lambda_{\pm 1}
part, by
[
P3
, Lemma 4.1, Lemma 4.8 and Corollary 4.14.]
, we know that

•

If
\pi_{\Lambda}
is a unipotent representation of a symplectic group or a special odd orthogonal group, then
\pi_{\Lambda}
is unique determined by
\pi_{\Lambda}^{\#}
;

•

If
\pi_{\Lambda}
is a unipotent representation of a even orthogonal group, then
\pi_{\Lambda}^{\#}=\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{\#}
if and only if
\Lambda^{\prime}\in\{\Lambda,\Lambda^{t}\}
.

•

If
\pi_{\Lambda,\iota}
is a unipotent representation of a odd orthogonal group, then
\pi_{\Lambda,\iota}^{\#}=\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime},\iota^{\prime}}^{\#}
if and only if
\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda
.

In summary, we describe the ambiguity of Lusztig correspondences as follows.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}^{\star}
be two different choice of Lusztig correspondences from
{\mathcal{E}}(G,s)
to
{\mathcal{E}}(C_{G}(s),1)
. Let
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{E}}(G,s)
. Assume that
\pi^{\prime}\in[\pi]
, and

\pi=\pi^{{\mathcal{L}}_{s}}_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},\iota}=\pi^{{\mathcal{L}}^{\star}_{s}}_{\rho^{\star},\Lambda_{1}^{\star},\Lambda_{-1}^{\star},\iota^{\star}}
and
\pi^{\prime}=\pi^{{\mathcal{L}}_{s}}_{\rho^{\prime},\Lambda_{1}^{\prime},\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime},\iota^{\prime}}
.

(1)

If
G
is a symplectic group, then

•

\rho^{\prime}=\rho^{\star}=\rho
;

•

\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}=\Lambda^{\star}=\Lambda_{1}
;

•

\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime},\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}\in\{\Lambda_{-1},\Lambda_{-1}^{t}\}
.

(2)

If
G
is a even orthogonal group, then

•

\rho^{\prime}=\rho^{\star}=\rho
;

•

\Lambda_{1}^{\prime},\Lambda_{1}^{\star}\in\{\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{1}^{t}\}
;

•

\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime},\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}\in\{\Lambda_{-1},\Lambda_{-1}^{t}\}
.

(3)

If
G
is a odd orthogonal group, then

•

\rho^{\prime}=\rho
;

•

\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}=\Lambda_{1}^{\star}=\Lambda_{1}
;

•

\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime}=\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}=\Lambda_{-1}.

Combining Proposition
3.4
and
[
P3
, Theorem 1.1]
, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
be a Lusztig correspondences from
{\mathcal{E}}(G,s)
to
{\mathcal{E}}(C_{G}(s),1)
, and
\pi\in{\mathcal{E}}(G,s)
. Assume that
\pi=\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},\iota}
.

(1)

For a symplectic group
G
, we have
\pi^{c}=\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}^{t}}
.

(2)

For a odd orthogonal group
G
, we have
\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi=\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},-\iota}
.

3.3.
Harish-Chandra series

Let
G
be a reductive group defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
,
F
be the corresponding Frobenius endomorphism, and let
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cus}}(G)
be the set of irreducible cuspidal representations of
G^{F}
.
A parabolic subgroup
P=LV
of
G
is the normalizer in
G
of a parabolic subgroup
P^{\circ}
of the connected component
G^{\circ}
of
G
.
The Levi subgroup
L
of
P
is the normalizer in
G
of the a Levi subgroup
L^{\circ}
of
P^{\circ}
. We say that a pair
(L,\delta)
is cuspidal if
L
is a Levi subgroup of an
F-
stable parabolic subgroup, and
\delta\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cus}}(L)
.

It is well know that for
\pi\in\mathcal{E}(G)
, there is a unique cuspidal pair
(L,\delta)
up to
G^{F}
-conjugacy such that
\langle\pi,{\mathrm{Ind}}_{L^{F}}^{G^{F}}(\delta)\rangle_{G^{F}}\neq 0
, where we lift
\delta
to a representation of
P^{F}
by making it trivial on
V^{F}
.
Thus we get a partition of
\mathcal{E}(G)
into series parametrized by
G^{F}
-conjugacy classes of cuspidal pairs
(L,\delta)
.
We focus on classical groups, and let
L
be an
F
-stable standard Levi subgroup of
G_{n}:=\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}
,
\mathrm{O}^{\pm}_{2n}
or
\mathrm{O}_{2n+1}
. Then
L
has a standard form

L=\mathrm{GL}_{n_{1}}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{2}}\times\cdots\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{r}}\times G_{m}

where
G_{m}=\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}
,
\mathrm{O}^{\pm}_{2m}
or
\mathrm{O}_{2m+1}
, and
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{r}+m=n
. For a cuspidal pair
(L,\delta)
, one has

\delta=\tau_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\tau_{r}\otimes\sigma

where
\tau_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cus}}(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}})
and
\sigma\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cus}}(G_{m})
.
Let

\cal{E}(G_{n},\sigma)=\{\pi\in\cal{E}(G_{n})|\textrm{ the cuspidal pair $(L,\delta)$ of $\pi$ is of form }\delta=\tau_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\tau_{r}\otimes\sigma\}.

Then we have a disjoint union

\mathcal{E}(G_{n})=\bigcup_{\sigma}\cal{E}(G_{n},\sigma),

where
\sigma
runs over all irreducible cuspidal representations of
G^{F}_{m}
,
m=0,1,\cdots,n
.

Let us consider the unipotent representation
\pi_{\Lambda}
of an even orthogonal group
G
. If
\mathrm{def}(\Lambda)\neq 0
, then
\pi_{\Lambda}
and
\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi_{\Lambda}=\pi_{\Lambda^{t}}
will belong to two distinct Harish-Chandra series
\cal{E}(G,\sigma)
and
\cal{E}(G,\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\sigma)
, respectively. On the other hand, if
\mathrm{def}(\Lambda)=0
, the cuspidal pairs of
\pi_{\Lambda}
and
\pi_{\Lambda^{t}}
will be the same. Therefore, to differentiate between
\pi_{\Lambda}
and
\pi_{\Lambda^{t}}
, we require a generalization of cuspidal representation, which is known as basic representation (or basic character according to
[
P3
]
).

Definition 3.6
.

Let
\pi\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)
be an irreducible representation of a symplectic or an orthogonal group. Fix a Lusztig correspondence
{\cal{L}}_{s}
. Assume that

\mathcal{L}_{s}(\pi)=\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\otimes\iota

where
\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
only occurs for odd orthogonal group. We say
\pi
is a basic representation if the following conditions are satisfied.

•

\rho
is cuspidal and
G^{*}_{[\neq 1]}(s)
is a product of unitary groups;

•

\pi_{\Lambda_{\pm 1}}
is either an irreducible unipotent cuspidal representation or
{\bf 1}_{\mathrm{O}^{+}_{2}}
,
\mathrm{sgn}_{\mathrm{O}^{+}_{2}}
.

Denote by
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{bas}}(G)
the set of irreducible basic representations of
G^{F}
.
We generalize the notations
\cal{E}(G_{n},\sigma)
and
(L,\delta)
in the same way and refer to
(L,\delta)
as the basic pair of
\pi
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\pi_{\Lambda}
be a unipotent representation of a even orthogonal group
G=\mathrm{O}_{2n}^{+}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Assume that
\mathrm{def}(\Lambda)=0
and
\Lambda\neq\Lambda^{t}
. Then
\pi_{\Lambda}
either lie in
\mathcal{E}(G,{\bf 1}_{\mathrm{O}^{+}_{2}})
or
\mathcal{E}(G,\mathrm{sgn}_{\mathrm{O}^{+}_{2}})
.

Proof.

It immediately follows from
[
P3
, Lemma 3.18]
.
∎

3.4.
Union of Lusztig correspondences

To construct the canonical Lusztig correspondence, we must ensure compatibility between Lusztig series of all groups of the same type, not just consider its behavior in a single Lusztig series.
Consider
G_{n}=\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}
,
\mathrm{O}_{2n+1}
and
\mathrm{O}^{\pm}_{2n}
.
Let

\cal{L}:\bigcup_{n}\mathcal{E}(G_{n})\to\bigcup_{n,(s)}\cal{E}((G_{n})^{*}_{[\neq\pm 1]}(s)\times(G_{n})^{*}_{[1]}(s)\times(G_{n})^{*}_{[-1]}(s),1)\times\begin{cases}\{\pm\},&\textrm{if }G_{n}=\mathrm{O}_{2n+1};\\
1,&\textrm{otherwise},\end{cases}

such that for
\pi\in\cal{E}(G,s)
, the map
\cal{L}
agrees with a Lusztig correspondence
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}
.

It is natural to require that Lusztig correspondence
\cal{L}
is compatible with parabolic induction. To be more precise, this compatibility means that the Lusztig correspondence
\cal{L}
satisfies the following conditions. Let
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},\iota}^{{\mathcal{L}}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(G_{n},\pi_{\rho^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}_{1},\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1},\iota^{\prime}}^{{\mathcal{L}}})
, where
\pi_{\rho^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}_{1},\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1},\iota^{\prime}}^{{\mathcal{L}}}
is a basic representation of
G_{m}^{F}
. Then we have

(i)

\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}
appears in the Harish-Chandra series of
\pi_{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}}
;

(ii)

\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}
appears in the Harish-Chandra series of
\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime}}
;

(iii)

Assume that
(L,\delta)
is the basic pair of
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},\iota}^{{\mathcal{L}}}
. Then
\iota=\delta(-I)
.

It is worth pointing out that if
\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{\pm 1})\neq 0
, the conditions (i) and (ii) are equal to
\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{\pm 1})=\mathrm{def}(\Lambda^{\prime}_{\pm 1})
.
The compatibility of unipotent representations is established by Proposition
2.2
. Proposition
3.4
tells us that the Lusztig correspondence can be characterized by
\iota
and
\Lambda^{\prime}_{\pm 1}
. Therefore, to create the canonical Lusztig correspondence with the aforementioned compatibility, we only need to construct it using basic representations.

We will now select a specific Lusztig correspondence, denoted by
\cal{L}
, for each
\{G_{n}\}
. We will use
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},\iota}
(or
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}}
) to represent
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},\iota}^{\cal{L}}
(or
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}}^{{\mathcal{L}}}
), respectively. If either
\rho
or
\Lambda_{\pm}
is trivial, we will ignore them in the subscript. For instance, if
\Lambda_{\pm 1}
is trivial, we will denote
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}}
as
\pi_{\rho}
. This notation coincides with
\pi_{\Lambda}
or
\pi_{\Lambda,\iota}
, which were defined in Section
2.4
, for unipotent representations.
It is important to note that
\cal{L}
is not the canonical choice of Lusztig correspondence that we aim to construct. We are using
{\mathcal{L}}
only for the sake of convenience in our statements and proofs.

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n})
. Then
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}}(-I)=\iota_{\rho}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})\varepsilon(-1)^{\mathrm{rank}(\Lambda_{-1})}
.

Proof.

The quadratic unipotent case is proved in
[
Wal2
, Proposition 4.11]
. The proof of the general case is similar.
∎

4.
Theta correspondence for finite symplectic groups and
finite orthogonal groups

Assume that
\mathbb{F}_{q}
a finite field of odd characteristic.
In this section we review the Theta correspondence of irreducible representations for finite symplectic groups and
finite orthogonal groups. We first recall the Theta correspondence for even orthogonal-symplectic
dual pairs. Then we deduce the Theta correspondence odd orthogonal-symplectic dual pairs from even orthogonal-symplectic
dual pairs.

4.1.
Isometry groups

Let
V_{\mathfrak{n}}
be an
{\mathfrak{n}}
-dimensional
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-vector space endowed with a
\epsilon
-symmetric form
\langle,\rangle_{V_{{\mathfrak{n}}}}
, i.e.
\langle v,w\rangle_{V_{{\mathfrak{n}}}}=\epsilon\langle w,v\rangle_{V_{{\mathfrak{n}}}}
for any
v,w\in V_{{\mathfrak{n}}}
.
The isometry group is a symlectic group or a orthogonal group.

For a symmetric space
V_{\mathfrak{n}}
, define its discriminant by

(4.1)

{\mathrm{disc}}(V_{\mathfrak{n}})=(-1)^{\frac{{\mathfrak{n}}({\mathfrak{n}}-1)}{2}}\det(V_{\mathfrak{n}})\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}/\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times 2}.

Write
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}
to be the isometry group of
V_{\mathfrak{n}}
with
{\mathrm{disc}}(V_{\mathfrak{n}})=\epsilon
. For even
{\mathfrak{n}}
, the even orthogonal group
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}
is split if and only if
\epsilon=+
. For odd
{\mathfrak{n}}
, we have
\mathrm{O}^{+}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}
and
\mathrm{O}^{-}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}
are the same as abstract groups; however they act on two symmetric spaces with different discriminants.

For a symmetric space
V_{\mathfrak{n}}
, the spinor norm
\chi_{\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{n}}}
of
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is a homomorphism from
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
to
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}/(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times})^{2}
defined as follows. Let
v\in V_{{\mathfrak{n}}}
be such that
\langle v,v\rangle_{V_{{\mathfrak{n}}}}\neq 0
and
s_{v}
be the reflection with respect to the hyperplane orthogonal to
v
, then
\chi_{\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{n}}}(s_{v})=\langle v,v\rangle_{V_{{\mathfrak{n}}}}\textrm{ mod }(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times})^{2}
. The value of
\chi_{\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{n}}}(-I_{\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{n}}})
has been calculated in
[
Wal1
, p,10]
, where
I_{\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{n}}}
is the identity of
{\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{\mathfrak{n}}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. In particular, we have

(4.2)

\chi_{\mathrm{O}^{+}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}}(-I_{\mathrm{O}^{+}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}})=-\chi_{\mathrm{O}^{-}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}}(-I_{\mathrm{O}^{-}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}}).

4.2.
Weil representation

We briefly recall the Theta correspondence over finite fields.

Let

V_{\mathfrak{n}}
and
V^{\prime}_{{{\mathfrak{n}}}^{\prime}}
be linear spaces over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
equipped with the
\epsilon
-symmetric form
\langle,\rangle_{V_{\mathfrak{n}}}
and
(-\epsilon)
-symmetric form
\langle,\rangle_{V^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime}}}
, respectively,
where
\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
. Let
G
and
G^{\prime}
be the isometry groups of
V_{\mathfrak{n}}
and
V^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime}}
,
respectively. Let
W:=V_{\mathfrak{n}}\otimes V^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime}}
be a symplectic equipped with the symplectic form
\langle,\rangle_{W}:=\langle,\rangle_{V_{\mathfrak{n}}}\otimes\langle,\rangle_{V^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime}}}
, and
\mathrm{Sp}(W)
be the isometry group of
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{W}
.
Then
(G,G^{\prime})
is a reductive dual pair inside
\mathrm{Sp}(W)
.

Let
\psi
be a nontrivial additive character of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.
Let
\omega_{N,\psi}
be the Weil representation (cf.
[
Ger
,
H
]
) of the finite symplectic group
\mathrm{Sp}(W)^{F}=\mathrm{Sp}_{2N}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
(with
\dim W=2N
), which affords the unique irreducible representation of
the Heisenberg group of
W
with central character
\psi
.
Write

\omega_{G\times G^{\prime}}^{\psi}
for the restriction of
\omega_{N,\psi}
to
G^{F}\times G^{\prime F}
. It is more convenience for us to use Ma-Qiu-Zou’s modified Weil representation
\omega_{G,G^{\prime}}^{\psi}
(
[
MQZ
, Section 1.4]
):

(4.3)

\omega_{G,G^{\prime}}^{\psi}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left({\bf 1}_{G}\otimes(\xi\circ\mathrm{det}_{V^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime}}})^{\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{dim}V_{{\mathfrak{n}}}}\right)\otimes\omega_{G\times G^{\prime}}^{\psi}&\textrm{ if $G^{\prime}$ is a orthogonal group};\\
\left((\xi\circ\mathrm{det}_{V_{{\mathfrak{n}}}})^{\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{dim}V^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime}}}\otimes{\bf 1}_{G^{\prime}}\right)\otimes\omega_{G\times G^{\prime}}^{\psi}&\textrm{ if $G$ is a orthogonal group},\end{array}\right.

where
{\bf 1}_{G}
is the trivial representation of
G^{F}
, and
\xi
is the unique quadratic character of
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
:

\xi:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}\to\{\pm 1\},\ a\to a^{\frac{q-1}{2}}.

We decomposes
\omega_{G,G^{\prime}}^{\psi}
into a direct sum

\omega_{G,G^{\prime}}^{\psi}=\bigoplus_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}m^{\psi}_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}\,\pi\otimes\pi^{\prime}

where
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
run over
{\mathcal{E}}(G)
and
{\mathcal{E}}(G^{\prime})
respectively, and
m^{\psi}_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}
are nonnegative integers. We can reassemble this decomposition as

\omega_{G,G^{\prime}}^{\psi}=\bigoplus_{\pi}\pi\otimes\Theta_{G,G^{\prime}}^{\psi}(\pi)

where
\Theta_{G,G^{\prime}}^{\psi}(\pi):=\bigoplus_{\pi^{\prime}}m^{\psi}_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}\pi^{\prime}
is a (not necessarily irreducible) representation of
G^{\prime F}
, called the (big) theta lift of
\pi
from
G^{F}
to
G^{\prime F}
. We will write
\pi^{\prime}\subset\Theta^{\psi}_{G,G^{\prime}}(\pi)
if
\pi\otimes\pi^{\prime}
occurs in
\omega^{\psi}_{G,G^{\prime}}
, i.e.
m^{\psi}_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}\neq 0
. We remark that even if
\Theta^{\psi}_{G,G^{\prime}}(\pi)=\pi^{\prime}
is irreducible, in general one only has

\pi\subset\Theta^{\psi}_{G^{\prime},G}(\pi^{\prime}),

where the equality does not necessarily hold.

Let
\psi
and
\psi^{\prime}
be two nontrivial additive characters of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
that belong to different square classes.
For the dual pair
(G,G^{\prime})=(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n^{\prime}+1})
, we denote
\omega_{G,G^{\prime}}^{\psi}
by
\omega^{\psi,\epsilon}_{n,n^{\prime}}
.
By
[
Sze
, Proposition 11]
, we have
\omega^{\psi,\epsilon}_{n,n^{\prime}}\cong\omega^{\psi^{\prime},-\epsilon}_{n,n^{\prime}}
.
Similar notation applies for
(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n^{\prime}})
. In this case, by the proof of Lemma 5.3 in
[
P3
]
, we know that

m^{\psi}_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}=m^{\psi}_{\pi^{c},(\pi^{\prime})^{c}}=m^{\psi^{\prime}}_{\pi,(\pi^{\prime})^{c}}=m^{\psi^{\prime}}_{\pi^{c},\pi^{\prime}},

where
\pi\in{\mathcal{E}}(G)
,
\pi^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{E}}(G^{\prime})
. Although we consider Ma-Qiu-Zou’s modified Weil representation instead of the original one, Pan’s proof still holds.

From now on, we fix a nontrivial additive character
\psi
of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and remove the superscript
\psi
in
\omega^{\psi,\epsilon}_{n,n^{\prime}}
. In particular, by considering the dual pair
(\mathrm{Sp}_{2N},\mathrm{O}^{+}_{1})
, we have
\omega_{N,\psi}\cong\omega^{+}_{N,1}
, and we simply denote it by
\omega_{N}^{+}
.

We write
{\bf G}={\bf Sp}
,
{\bf O}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{even}}
and
{\bf O}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{odd}}
for the Witt tower
\{G_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}=\{\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}\}_{n\geq 0}
,
\{\mathrm{O}^{\pm}_{2n}\}_{n\geq 0}
and
\{\mathrm{O}^{\pm}_{2n+1}\}_{n\geq 0}
.
Abbreviate the theta lifting from
G_{n}
to
G^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
as
\Theta^{\pm}_{n,n^{\prime}}
or
\Theta
when the dual pairs’ context is clear.

4.3.
A explicit description of the theta correspondence

In their paper
[
AMR
]
, Aubert, Michel, and Rouquier proposed a conjecture that explicitly describes the theta correspondence. Pan proved this conjecture in subsequent papers
[
P1
,
P2
]
. However, they studied Gérardin’s Weil representation, whereas we consider Ma-Qiu-Zou’s Weil representation in this paper because it is compatible with parabolic induction (cf.
[
MQZ
, 1.13]
). This means that if
\sigma^{\prime}
appears in the theta lifting of
\sigma
with
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cus}}(G_{n^{\prime}})^{\prime}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}_{cus}(G_{n})
for a dual pair
(G_{n},G_{n^{\prime}}^{\prime})
with
G_{n}\in{\bf G}
and
G_{n^{\prime}}^{\prime}\in{\bf G^{\prime}}
, then the irreducible components of the theta lifts of elements in the Harish-Chandra series
\mathcal{E}(G_{m},\sigma)
lie in the Harish-Chandra series
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cus}}(G^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}},\sigma^{\prime})
. It is worth mentioning that Pan’s study was based on compatibility with parabolic induction, as described above. Therefore, the results in
[
P1
,
P2
]
actually apply to Ma-Qiu-Zou’s Weil representation, not Gérardin’s. Moreover, Theorem 1.4 in
[
MQZ
]
shows that Aubert, Michel, and Rouquier’s conjecture holds for Ma-Qiu-Zou’s Weil representation. As a consequence, all results in
[
Wang1
]
are for Ma-Qiu-Zou’s Weil representation, not Gérardin’s.

The next result shows that the theta lifting and the parabolic induction are compatible.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
G_{n}
and
G_{n+\ell}
be two classical groups in the same Witt tower of symplectic groups or orthogonal groups,
\ell\geq 0
. Let
\pi\in\cal{E}(G_{n},s)
with
s\in(G^{*}_{n})^{\circ}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, and
\pi^{\prime}:=\Theta_{n,n^{\prime}}(\pi)
. Let
\chi_{\mathrm{GL}_{\ell}}
be the unique linear character of
\mathrm{GL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
of order
2
. Let
\tau\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{GL}_{\ell},s_{0})
with
s_{0}\in\mathrm{GL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Let
\mathrm{Ind}^{G_{n+\ell}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}_{\mathrm{GL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\times G_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}(\tau\otimes\pi)=\bigoplus_{i}\rho_{i}
with
\rho_{i}
irreducible. Assume that
s
and
s_{0}
have no common eigenvalues, and
s_{0}
has no eigenvalues
\pm 1
. Then we have

\bigoplus_{i}\Theta_{n+\ell,n^{\prime}+\ell}(\rho_{i})=\mathrm{Ind}^{G^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}+\ell}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}_{\mathrm{GL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\times G^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}((\chi\otimes\tau)\otimes\pi^{\prime}).

where

\chi=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\chi_{\mathrm{GL}_{\ell}},&\textrm{if $(G_{n+\ell},G^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}+\ell})$ contains an odd orthogonal group,}\\
1,&\textrm{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Hence, by abuse of notations, we write

\Theta_{n+\ell,n^{\prime}+\ell}\left(\mathrm{Ind}^{G_{n+\ell}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}_{\mathrm{GL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\times G_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}(\tau\otimes\pi)\right)=\mathrm{Ind}^{G^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}+\ell}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}_{\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{F}_{q})_{\ell}\times G^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}((\chi\otimes\tau)\otimes\pi^{\prime}).

The theta lifting of unipotent representations is as follows.

Theorem 4.2

(Pan)
.

Let
\pi_{\Lambda}\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},1)
and
\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}}\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n^{\prime}},1)
. Then
\pi_{\Lambda}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
occurs in
\omega_{n,n^{\prime}}^{\epsilon}
if and only if
(\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}^{\epsilon}_{n,n^{\prime}}
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{+}_{n,n^{\prime}}:=\left\{(\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime})|{}^{t}(\Upsilon(\Lambda^{\prime})^{-})\preccurlyeq{}^{t}(\Upsilon(\Lambda)^{+}),{}^{t}(\Upsilon(\Lambda)^{-})\preccurlyeq{}^{t}(\Upsilon(\Lambda^{\prime})^{+}),\mathrm{def}(\Lambda^{\prime})=-\mathrm{def}(\Lambda)+1\right\};

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{-}_{n,n^{\prime}}:=\left\{(\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime})|{}^{t}(\Upsilon(\Lambda^{\prime})^{+})\preccurlyeq{}^{t}(\Upsilon(\Lambda)^{-}),{}^{t}(\Upsilon(\Lambda)^{+})\preccurlyeq{}^{t}(\Upsilon(\Lambda^{\prime})^{-}),\mathrm{def}(\Lambda^{\prime})=-\mathrm{def}(\Lambda)-1\right\}

are two subsets of
\cal{S}_{n}\times\cal{S}_{n^{\prime}}^{+}
and
\cal{S}_{n}\times\cal{S}_{n^{\prime}}^{-}
, respectively.

The general case can be deduce from the unipotent case for dual pair
(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n^{\prime}})
by Theorem
4.3
and Theorem
4.4
.

Theorem 4.3

(Pan)
.

Let
(G,G^{\prime})=(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n^{\prime}})
, and let
\pi\in\cal{E}(G,s)
and
\pi^{\prime}\in\cal{E}(G^{\prime},s^{\prime})
for
some semisimple elements
s\in G^{*}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
and
s^{\prime}\in(G^{\prime*})^{0}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Write
\cal{L}_{s}(\pi)=\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}
and
and
\cal{L}_{s^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime})=\rho^{\prime}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1}}
.
Then there exists an irreducible representation
\pi^{\prime\prime}\in[\pi^{\prime}]

such that
\pi\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}
occurs in
\omega_{n,n^{\prime}}^{\epsilon}
if and only if the following conditions hold:

•

For any
a\neq\pm 1
in
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
,
G^{*}_{[a]}(s)\cong G^{\prime*}_{[a]}(s^{\prime})
, and
\pi[a]\cong\pi^{\prime}[a]
, where
G^{*}_{[\neq\pm 1]}(s)=\prod G^{*}_{[a]}(s)
,
G^{\prime*}_{[\neq\pm 1]}(s^{\prime})=\prod G^{\prime*}_{[a]}(s^{\prime})
,

\rho=\prod\pi[a]
, and
\rho^{\prime}=\prod\pi^{\prime}[a]
;

•

either
\pi^{\mathrm{Sp}}_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}
or
\pi^{\mathrm{Sp}}_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}^{t}}
occurs in
\omega_{G^{*}_{[1]}(s),G^{\prime*}_{[1]}(s^{\prime})}
;

•

\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\in[\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1}}]
,

where
\pi^{\mathrm{Sp}}_{\Lambda_{1}}:={\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\pi_{\Lambda_{1}})
is the unipotent representation of a symplectic group correspondence to the symbol
\Lambda_{1}
.
That is, the following diagram:

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

commutes up to a twist of the sgn character.

Theorem 4.4

(Pan)
.

Let
(G,G^{\prime})=(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n^{\prime}+1})
, and let
\pi\in\cal{E}(G,s)
and
\pi^{\prime}\in\cal{E}(G^{\prime},s^{\prime})
for some semisimple elements
s\in G^{*}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
and
s^{\prime}\in(G^{\prime*})^{0}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Write
\cal{L}_{s}(\pi)=\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}
and
and
\cal{L}_{s^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime})=\rho^{\prime}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1}}\otimes\epsilon^{\prime}
. Then there exists an irreducible representation
\pi^{\prime\prime}\in[\pi^{\prime}]
such that
\pi\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}
occurs in
\omega_{n,n^{\prime}}^{\epsilon}
if and only if the following conditions hold:

•

For any
a\neq\pm 1
in
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
,
G^{*}_{[a]}(s)\cong G^{\prime*}_{[-a]}(s^{\prime})
, and
\pi[a]\cong\pi^{\prime}[-a]
, where
G^{*}_{[\neq\pm 1]}(s)=\prod G^{*}_{[a]}(s)
,
G^{\prime*}_{[\neq\pm 1]}(s^{\prime})=\prod G^{\prime*}_{[a]}(s^{\prime})
,

\rho=\prod\pi[a]
, and
\rho^{\prime}=\prod\pi^{\prime}[a]
;

•

either
\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}}
or
\pi_{\Lambda^{t}_{-1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}}
occurs in
\omega_{G^{*}_{[-1]}(s),G^{\prime*}_{[1]}(s^{\prime})}
;

•

\pi^{\mathrm{Sp}}_{\Lambda_{1}}\cong\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1}}
,

where
\pi^{\mathrm{Sp}}_{\Lambda_{1}}:={\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\pi_{\Lambda_{1}})
is the unipotent representation of a symplectic group correspondence to the symbol
\Lambda_{1}
.
That is, the following diagram:

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

commutes up to a twist of the sgn character where
f(\rho^{\prime}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1}}\otimes\epsilon^{\prime})=\rho^{\prime}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}}
.

Therefore, the description of general Howe correspondence for dual pair of a symplectic group and an orthogonal group is now completely characterized up to sgn.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\pi\in{\mathcal{E}}(G)
, and
\pi^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{E}}(G^{\prime})
, and let
I_{G}
and
I_{G^{\prime}}
be the identities of
G
and
G^{\prime}
, respectively. Assume that
\pi\otimes\pi^{\prime}
occurs in the Weil representation
\omega_{G,G^{\prime}}^{\psi}
.

(i)

If
(G,G^{\prime})=(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n^{\prime}})
, then
\pi(-I_{G})=\pi^{\prime}(-I_{G^{\prime}})
.

(ii)

If
(G,G^{\prime})=(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n^{\prime}})
, then
\pi(-I_{G})=\varepsilon(-1)^{n}\cdot\pi^{\prime}(-I_{G^{\prime}})
.

Proof.

It immediately follows from the fact that the embedding
G\times G^{\prime}\to\mathrm{Sp}_{2N}
sends both
-I_{G}\times I_{G^{\prime}}
and
I_{G}\times-I_{G^{\prime}}
to
-I_{\mathrm{Sp}_{2N}}
. Note that we have a character
(\xi\circ\mathrm{det})^{n}
in Ma-Qiu-Zou’s Weil representation (
4.3
). So we need to add
\varepsilon(-1)^{n}
when
(G,G^{\prime})=(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n^{\prime}+1})
.
∎

4.4.
First occurrence index of unipotent basic representations

For an irreducible representation
\pi
of
G_{n}
, the smallest integer
n(\pi)
such that
\pi
occurs in
\omega_{G_{n},G^{\prime}_{n(\pi)}}
is called the
first occurrence index
of
\pi
with respect to the Witt tower
\left\{G^{\prime}_{n}\right\}_{\geq 0}
, and called
\Theta_{G_{n},G^{\prime}_{n(\pi)}}
the first theta lifting of
\pi
.

Definition 4.6
.

(1)

Assume that
G_{n}=\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}
. Let
A\in\{\mathrm{even},\mathrm{odd}\}
, and
n^{\epsilon}_{A}(\pi)
be the first occurrence index of
\pi
with respect to the Witt tower
{\bf O}^{\epsilon}_{A}
. We set

{\bf n}^{\epsilon}_{A}(\pi):=n-n^{\epsilon}_{A}(\pi).

We abbreviate
n^{\epsilon}_{\mathrm{even}}(\pi)
,
n^{\epsilon}_{\mathrm{odd}}(\pi)
,
{\bf n}^{\epsilon}_{\mathrm{even}}(\pi)
and
{\bf n}^{\epsilon}_{\mathrm{odd}}(\pi)
by
n^{\epsilon}(\pi)
and
{\bf n}^{\epsilon}(\pi)
when no confusion arise.

(2)

Assume that
G_{n}=\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n}
or
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n+1}
. Let
n^{\epsilon}(\pi)
be the first occurrence index of
\pi
with respect to the Witt tower
{\bf Sp}
. We set

{\bf n}^{\epsilon}(\pi):=n-n^{\epsilon}(\pi).

(3)

We say that the theta correspondence of
\pi
goes down (resp. up) with respect to the Witt tower
\left\{G^{\prime}_{n}\right\}
if
{\bf n}^{\epsilon}(\pi)\geq 0
(resp.
{\bf n}^{\epsilon}(\pi)<0
).

For a dual pair
(G_{n},G^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})
and an irreducible cuspidal representation
\pi
of
G_{n}
, by
[
AM
, Theorem 2.2]
, there is a cuspidal representation appearing in the theta lifting if and only if
n^{\prime}
is the first occurrence index of
\pi
. Moreover, the theta lifting of
\pi
is an irreducible cuspidal representation.

Theorem 4.7

(
[
AM
]
, Theorem 5.2)
.

The theta correspondence for dual pairs
(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n^{\prime}})
takes unipotent cuspidal representations to unipotent cuspidal representations as follows :

(1)

(\mathrm{Sp}_{2k(k+1)},\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2k^{2}})
,
\epsilon=\mathrm{sgn}(-1)^{k}
,

\Theta^{\epsilon}_{k(k+1),k^{2}}:\left\{\begin{aligned} &\pi_{\Lambda},\ \Lambda=\begin{pmatrix}2k,\cdots,1,0\\
-\end{pmatrix}\to\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}},\ \Lambda^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}-\\
2k-1,\cdots,1,0\\
\end{pmatrix}\textrm{ if }k\textrm{ is even;}\\
&\pi_{\Lambda},\ \Lambda=\begin{pmatrix}-\\
2k,\cdots,1,0\end{pmatrix}\to\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}},\ \Lambda^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}2k-1,\cdots,1,0\\
-\end{pmatrix}\textrm{ if }k\textrm{ is odd}.\end{aligned}\right.

(2)

(\mathrm{Sp}_{2k(k+1)},\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2(k+1)^{2}})
,
\epsilon=\mathrm{sgn}(-1)^{k+1}
,

\Theta^{\epsilon}_{k(k+1),(k+1)^{2}}:\left\{\begin{aligned} &\pi_{\Lambda},\ \Lambda=\begin{pmatrix}2k,\cdots,1,0\\
-\end{pmatrix}\to\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}},\ \Lambda^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}-\\
2k+1,\cdots,1,0\\
\end{pmatrix}\textrm{ if }k\textrm{ is even;}\\
&\pi_{\Lambda},\ \Lambda=\begin{pmatrix}-\\
2k,\cdots,1,0\end{pmatrix}\to\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}},\ \Lambda^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}2k+1,\cdots,1,0\\
-\end{pmatrix}\textrm{ if }k\textrm{ is odd}.\end{aligned}\right.

Proposition 4.8
.

(
[
P3
, Example 5.5 (1)]
)
The first occurrence index of
{\bf 1}_{\mathrm{O}^{+}_{2}}
and
\mathrm{sgn}_{\mathrm{O}^{+}_{2}}
is 0 and 1, respectively.

Corollary 4.9
.

(1)

Let
\pi_{\Lambda}
be the unipotent cuspidal representation of
\mathrm{Sp}_{2k(k+1)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Then

{\bf n}^{\epsilon}_{\mathrm{even}}(\pi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-k-1&\textrm{if $\epsilon\cdot\mathrm{def}(\Lambda)<0$;}\\
k&\textrm{if $\epsilon\cdot\mathrm{def}(\Lambda)>0$}.\end{array}\right.

(2)

Let
\pi_{\Lambda}
be the unipotent basic representation of
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2k^{2}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
with
\epsilon=\mathrm{sgn}(-1)^{k+1}
. Then

{\bf n}^{\epsilon}(\pi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-k&\textrm{if $\epsilon\cdot\mathrm{def}(\Lambda)<0$;}\\
k&\textrm{if $\epsilon\cdot\mathrm{def}(\Lambda)\geq 0$}.\end{array}\right.

4.5.
Conservation relation for basic representations

In this subsection, we will differentiate between representations in
[\pi]
using theta correspondence. This will enable us to determine a Lusztig correspondence, as there is ambiguity in the Lusztig correspondence on
[\pi]
.

According to
[
P3
, Theorem 1.1]
, the set
[\pi]
consists of
\pi
,
\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi
,
\pi^{c}
and
\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi^{c}
, provided such representations exist. For even orthogonal groups, the first occurrence indexes
n^{\epsilon}(\pi)
and
n^{\epsilon}(\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi)
are different due to the conservation relation. To distinguish between
\pi
and
\pi^{c}
, we examine their conservation relation and show that although their first indexes are the same, their first theta lifting is different.
For odd orthogonal groups, we have two Witt towers
{\bf O}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{odd}}
, and we can prove that
n^{\epsilon}(\pi)\neq n^{\epsilon}(\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi)
for
\epsilon=\pm
. This allows us to differentiate between
\pi
and
\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi
using their first occurrence indexes. For symplectic groups, there is no sgn character. We give a conservation relation for
\pi
and
\pi^{c}
, and prove that
n^{\epsilon}_{\mathrm{odd}}(\pi)\neq n^{\epsilon}_{\mathrm{odd}}(\pi^{c})
for
\epsilon=\pm
.

Let
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},\iota}
be an irreducible cuspidal representation of
\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
\mathrm{O}^{\pm}_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
or
\mathrm{O}_{2n+1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, where
\iota\in\{\pm\}
only occurs for odd orthogonal groups. Assume that
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{-1}
correspond to unipotent basic representations of
G^{*}_{[1]}(s)
and
G^{*}_{[-1]}(s)
, respectively. Let

k=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{|\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{1})|-1}{2}&\textrm{ if }\Lambda_{1}\in\cal{S}_{m};\\
\frac{\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{1})}{2}&\textrm{ if }\Lambda_{1}\in\cal{S}^{\pm}_{m}\textrm{ and }\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{1})\neq 0;\\
0.5&\textrm{ if }\Lambda_{1}=\binom{1}{0};\\
-0.5&\textrm{ if }\Lambda_{1}=\binom{0}{1},\\
\end{array}\right.

and

h=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{|\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{-1})|-1}{2}&\textrm{ if }\Lambda_{-1}\in\cal{S}_{m^{\prime}};\\
\frac{\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{-1})}{2}&\textrm{ if }\Lambda_{-1}\in\cal{S}^{\pm}_{m^{\prime}}\textrm{ and }\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{-1})\neq 0;.\\
0.5&\textrm{ if }\Lambda_{-1}=\binom{1}{0};\\
-0.5&\textrm{ if }\Lambda_{-1}=\binom{0}{1}.\\
\end{array}\right.

For abbreviation, we write
\pi_{\rho,k,h,\iota}
instead of
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},\iota}
. By Corollary
3.5
, we have

\pi_{\rho,k,h}^{c}=\pi_{\rho,k,-h}
if
\pi_{\rho,k,h}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{cus}}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n})
, and
\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi_{\rho,k,h,\iota}=\pi_{\rho,k,h,-\iota}
if
\pi_{\rho,k,h,\iota}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{bas}}(\mathrm{O}_{2n+1})
.

The first occurrence indexes of cuspidal representations have been calculated in
[
Wang1
, Section 6]
. It is not difficult to extend these results to basic representations.

Proposition 4.10
.

[
Wang1
, Proposition 6.2]

Let
\pi_{\rho,k,h}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{bas}}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n})
. Then the following hold:

(i) Consider the Witt tower
{\bf O}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{even}}
. We have

•

(Conservation relation)
\{n^{\pm}(\pi_{\rho,k,h})\}=\{n^{\pm}(\pi_{\rho,k,-h})\}=\{n-k,n+k+1\}
;

•

(Conservation relation)
n^{\epsilon}(\pi_{\rho,k,h})=n^{\epsilon}(\pi_{\rho,k,-h})
and
\Theta_{n,n^{\epsilon}(\pi_{\rho,k,h})}(\pi_{\rho,k,h})\neq\Theta_{n,n^{\epsilon}(\pi_{\rho,k,-h})}(\pi_{\rho,k,-h})
;

•

if
\pi_{\rho,k,h}
goes down with respect to the Witt tower
{\bf O}^{\epsilon}_{\mathrm{even}}
, then
\Theta_{n,n^{\epsilon}(\pi_{\rho,k,h})}(\pi_{\rho,k,h})=\pi_{\rho,k^{\prime},h^{\prime}}
with
|k^{\prime}|=|k|
and
|h^{\prime}|=|h|
.

(ii) Consider the Witt tower
{\bf O}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{odd}}
. We have

•

(Conservation relation)
\{n^{\pm}(\pi_{\rho,k,h})\}=\{n^{\pm}(\pi_{\rho,k,-h})\}=\{\lfloor n\pm h\rfloor\}
;

•

if
\pi_{\rho,k,h}
goes down with respect to the Witt tower
{\bf O}^{\epsilon}_{\mathrm{odd}}
, then
\Theta_{n,n^{\epsilon}(\pi_{\rho,k,h})}(\pi_{\rho,k,h})=\pi_{\rho,k^{\prime},h^{\prime},\iota^{\prime}}
with
k^{\prime}=\lceil|h|-1\rceil
and
h^{\prime}=k
. Moreover, we have
\Theta_{n^{\epsilon}(\pi_{\rho,k,h}),n}(\pi_{\rho,k^{\prime},h^{\prime},\iota^{\prime}})=\pi_{\rho,k,h}
.

Proposition 4.11
.

[
Wang1
, Proposition 6.3]

(i) Let
\pi_{\rho,k,h}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{bas}}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n})
. Then the following hold:

•

(Conservation relation)
\{n^{\epsilon}(\pi_{\rho,k,h}),n^{\epsilon}(\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi_{\rho,k,h})\}\in\{\lfloor n\pm k\rfloor\}
;

•

(Conservation relation)
n^{\epsilon}(\pi_{\rho,k,h})=n^{\epsilon}(\pi_{\rho,k,h}^{c})
and
