0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where

•

\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
;

•

m=n-\ell
(resp.
m^{\prime}=n^{\prime}-\ell^{\prime}
) where
\ell
(resp.
\ell^{\prime}
) is the first descent index of
\pi
(resp.
\pi^{\prime}
);

•

{\mathcal{D}}_{\ell,\epsilon}
(resp.
{\mathcal{D}}_{\ell^{\prime},\epsilon}
) is
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{FJ}}_{\ell,\epsilon}
or
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{\ell,\epsilon}
, depends on
G_{n}
(resp.
G^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
);

•

\sigma^{*}
is the image of
\sigma
under
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{can}}
with
\sigma\in\{\pi,\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\star},\pi^{\star\prime}\}
;

•

n^{\prime}
(resp.
m^{\prime}
) is the (theta) first occurrence index of
\pi
(resp.
\pi^{\star}
);

•

If dual pair
(G_{n},G^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})
contains a even orthogonal group, then

\Theta_{n,n^{\prime}}^{*}:\pi^{*}=\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\to\pi^{\prime*}=\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}},

Here
\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}}
is the first theta lifting of
\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}
;

•

If dual pair
(G_{n},G^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})=(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n^{\prime}+1})
, then

\Theta_{n,n^{\prime}}^{*}:\pi^{*}=\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\to\pi^{\prime*}=\rho^{-}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})\varepsilon(-1)^{\mathrm{rank}(\Lambda_{1})}.

Here
\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}}
is the first theta lifting of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}&\textrm{ if }\varepsilon(-1)\epsilon\epsilon_{\rho}=+;\\
\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}&\textrm{ if }\varepsilon(-1)\epsilon\epsilon_{\rho}=-;\\
\end{array}\right.

•

If dual pair
(G_{n},G^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})=(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n+1},\mathrm{Sp}_{2n^{\prime}})
, then

\Theta_{n,n^{\prime}}^{*}:\pi^{*}=\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\otimes\iota\to\pi^{\prime*}=\rho^{-}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}}.

Here
\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}}

\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}}^{\prime}&\textrm{ if }\epsilon\epsilon_{\rho}=+;\\
\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}}^{\prime}&\textrm{ if }\epsilon\epsilon_{\rho}=-,\\
\end{array}\right.

and
\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}}^{\prime}
is the first theta lifting of
\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}
;

•

The map
\Theta_{m,m^{\prime}}
is defined in a similar way.

In order to construct
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{can}}
, we follow an inductive approach using
{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}
. We assume that the canonical Lusztig correspondence
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{can}}
in the bottom side of (
6.1
) and functors
{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}
have already been constructed.
The commutative diagram at the front side of (
6.1
), also known as the theta-descent commutative diagram, has been proven to be commutative in (
5.10
). To ensure the entire diagram (
6.1
) is commutative, we need to find the unique Lusztig correspondences
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{can}}
on
G_{n}
and
G^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
to make the left, right and top diagrams commutative, which will automatically result in the back side of (
6.1
) being commutative as well.

We set
{\mathcal{D}}_{\epsilon}^{*}
as follows. Recall that

(6.2)

\displaystyle C_{G_{n}^{*}}(s)=\,

\displaystyle(G_{n})^{*}_{[\neq\pm 1]}(s)\times(G_{n})^{*}_{[1]}(s)\times(G_{n})^{*}_{[-1]}(s)\times\begin{cases}\{\pm\},&\textrm{if }G=\mathrm{O}_{2n+1};\\
1,&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Assume that
G_{n}=\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}
, and
\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(C_{G_{n}^{*}}(s))
. Then we set

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}(\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}})=\begin{cases}\rho^{\star}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}&\textrm{ if }\epsilon=-\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1})\epsilon_{\rho}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}};\\
0&\textrm{ otherwise, }\end{cases}

where

•

\rho^{\star}=\rho^{-}_{1}
;

•

The symbol
\Lambda_{1}^{\star}
is obtained by (1) removing the maximal number in
\Lambda_{-1}
, (2) swapping the arrays of
\Lambda_{-1}
if
\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})=+
;

•

The symbol
\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}
is obtained by (1) removing the maximal number in
\Lambda_{1}
, (2) swapping the arrays of
\Lambda_{1}
if
\varepsilon(-1)\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})=+
, i.e. we have
\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda^{\star}_{-1})=-\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\varepsilon(-1)\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})
.

Assume that
G_{n}=\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n}
, and
\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(C_{G_{n}^{*}}(s))
. Then we set

{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}})=\begin{cases}\rho^{\star}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}\otimes\iota^{\star}&\textrm{ if }\epsilon^{\prime}=\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{1})\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1})\epsilon_{\rho}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}};\\
0&\textrm{ otherwise, }\end{cases}

where

•

\rho^{\star}=\rho_{1}
;

•

The symbol
\Lambda_{1}^{\star}
is obtained by (1) removing the maximal number in
\Lambda_{1}
, (2) swapping the arrays of
\Lambda_{1}
if
\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})=+
;

•

The symbol
\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}
is obtained by (1) removing the maximal number in
\Lambda_{-1}
, (2) swapping the arrays of
\Lambda_{-1}
if
\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})=+
;

•

\iota^{\star}=-{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho^{\star}}\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{1})
.

Assume that
G_{n}=\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n+1}
, and
\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\otimes\iota\in{\mathcal{E}}(C_{G_{n}^{*}}(s))
. Then we set

{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\iota)=\begin{cases}\rho^{\star}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}&\textrm{ if }\epsilon^{\prime}=\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})\epsilon\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}};\\
0&\textrm{ otherwise, }\end{cases}

where

•

\rho^{\star}=\rho_{1}
;

•

The symbol
\Lambda_{1}^{\star}
is obtained by (1) removing the maximal numbet in
\Lambda_{1}
, (2) swapping the arrays of
\Lambda_{1}
if
\iota{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})=-
, i.e.
\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{1}^{\star})=\iota{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho}
;

•

The symbol
\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}
is obtained by (1) removing the maximal numbet in
\Lambda_{-1}
, (2) swapping the arrays of
\Lambda_{-1}
if
\iota{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho}\epsilon\epsilon_{\rho}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\varepsilon(-1)=-
, i.e. we have
\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})=\iota{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho}\epsilon\epsilon_{\rho}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\varepsilon(-1)\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})
.

It is worth noting that although the construction of
{\mathcal{D}}_{\epsilon}^{*}
may seem artificial at first glance, there is a deeper reason behind it. Specifically, if the canonical Lusztig correspondence
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{can}}
exists and (
6.1
) is commutative, then
{\mathcal{D}}_{\epsilon}^{*}
must be of the same form as we have constructed it. To elaborate further, the commutativity of the left and right side of equation (
6.1
) implies that the functor
{\mathcal{D}}_{\epsilon}^{*}
must also possess the same characteristic as that of
{\mathcal{D}}_{\ell,\epsilon}
, which we previously established in Section
5.5
. This is precisely why the construction of
{\mathcal{D}}_{\epsilon}^{*}
involves signs like
\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{\pm})
and
\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{\pm})
.

6.3.
Proof of Theorem
6.1
for basic representations

We prove it by induction on
n
. We will only prove it on
\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. The proof for orthogonal groups side is similar and will be left to readers. Assume that we have already construct
\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}
on basic representations of
G_{n}
for
n<k
, and
\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}
satisfies the theta conditions (2) and (3) in Theorem
6.1
for basic representations of dual pair
(G_{n},G_{m})
with
m<k
.

Construction of
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
on
\pi
.

Assume that
\pi\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{bas}}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2k})
, and
\pi=\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}}
. Recall that different choices of Lusztig correspondence differ only on the
\Lambda_{-1}
part. If
\Lambda_{-1}
is the trivial symbol, then there is nothing to prove. So we assume that
\Lambda_{-1}
is not trivial.
Consider the first descent of
\pi
. Let
\ell
be the first descent index of
\pi
.
By Proposition
5.9
, we know that the first descents of basic representations are still basic. Assume that

{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{FJ}}_{\ell,\epsilon}(\pi)=\pi^{\star}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{cus}}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2k^{\star}}),

where
k^{\star}=k-\ell
. We know that the subscript
\epsilon
in
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{FJ}}_{\ell,\epsilon}
and the representation
\pi^{\star}
are determined by
\pi
, not the choice of Lusztig correspondence. Therefore we will construct
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
on
\pi
by them.
As mentioned in section
6.1
, we define
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
to be the Lusztig correspondence such that the left side of (
6.1
) is commutative,

\textstyle{\pi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{FJ}}_{\ell,\epsilon}}
\scriptstyle{{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{\pi_{\rho^{\star},\Lambda^{\star}_{1},\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\mathrm{can}}_{-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}}
\textstyle{\quad\rho^{\star}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}^{\star}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}}.}

Assume that
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}(\pi)=\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\mathrm{can}}_{-1}}
, where
\Lambda^{\mathrm{can}}_{-1}\in\{\Lambda_{-1},\Lambda^{t}_{-1}\}
.
Since
{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}({\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}(\pi))\neq 0
, we have

(6.3)

\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{can}}_{-1})=-\epsilon\epsilon_{\rho}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1}),

which determines the symbol
\Lambda^{\mathrm{can}}_{-1}
, and so is
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
.
By abuse of notations, from now on, we redefine
\Lambda_{-1}
to be
\Lambda^{\mathrm{can}}_{-1}
.

On the other hand, By our induction assumption, we have already construct
\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}
on
\pi^{\star}
. Write

{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}(\pi^{\star})=\rho^{\star}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}^{\star}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}}.

By our construction of
{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}
, if
\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}\neq\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-\\
-\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, the sign
\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})
is unique determined by
\pi
:

(6.4)

\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda^{\star}_{-1})=-\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\varepsilon(-1)\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})=\epsilon\epsilon_{\rho}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})\varepsilon(-1),

where the last equation follows from (
6.3
).
Therefore, to ensure that our construction of
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
on
\pi
does not contradict
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
on
\pi^{\star}
, we have to check (
6.4
), when
\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}\neq\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-\\
-\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Proof of (
6.4
).
From now on, let us forget (
6.3
), and try to obtain
\epsilon
and prove (
6.4
). Our strategy is sending
\pi
and
\pi^{\star}
to smaller orthogonal groups by Theta correspondence.
Let
k^{\pm}
be the first theta occurrence indexes of
\pi
with respect to the Witt towers
{\bf{{O}}}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{\pm}
. Recall that
\Lambda_{-1}
is not trivial. Then by Proposition
4.10
, there exists
k^{\epsilon^{\prime}}
such that
k^{\epsilon^{\prime}}<k
, and
\Theta^{\epsilon^{\prime}}_{k,k^{\epsilon^{\prime}}}(\pi)
is an irreducible basic representation, and we denote it by
\pi^{\prime}=\pi_{\rho^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}_{1},\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1},\iota^{\prime}}
.
By Proposition
4.10
, we also know that
\Theta^{\epsilon^{\prime}}_{k^{\epsilon^{\prime}},k}(\pi^{\prime})=\pi
. Similarly, let
k^{\star\pm}
be the first theta occurrence index of
\pi^{\star}
with respect to the Witt tower
{\bf{{O}}}_{\mathrm{even}}^{\pm}
, and denote the smaller one by
k^{\star\prime}
, and the correspondence group by
\mathrm{O}_{2k^{\star\prime}}^{\epsilon^{\star\prime}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Write
\Theta^{\epsilon^{\star\prime}}_{k^{\star},k^{\star\prime}}(\pi^{\star})=\pi_{\rho^{\star\prime},\Lambda^{\star\prime}_{1},\Lambda^{\star\prime}_{-1}}
.
By our induction assumption, we have the following theta commutative diagram for
\pi^{\star}
(the bottom diagram of (
6.1
)):

\textstyle{\pi^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Theta}
\scriptstyle{{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{\pi_{\rho^{\star\prime},\Lambda^{\star\prime}_{1},\Lambda^{\star\prime}_{-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{\rho^{\star}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Theta^{*}_{k^{\star},k^{\epsilon^{\star\prime}}}}
\textstyle{\quad\rho^{\star\prime}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}^{\star\prime}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}^{\star\prime}}.}

Since the Theta correspondence in the above diagram goes down, by Proposition
4.10
and Theorem
4.2
, we have

•

\rho^{\star\prime}=\rho^{\star}
;

•

\Lambda_{-1}^{\star\prime}=\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}
;

•

|\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{1}^{\star\prime})|=|\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{1}^{\star})|-1
, and
\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{1}^{\star\prime})=-\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1}^{\star})
.

Then the superscript
\epsilon^{\star\prime}
on
\mathrm{O}_{2k^{\star\prime}}^{\epsilon^{\star\prime}}
equals to
\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1}^{\star})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})
.

By (
5.10
), we have
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{\ell-1,\epsilon}(\pi^{\prime})=\pi_{\rho^{\star\prime},\Lambda^{\star\prime}_{1},\Lambda^{\star\prime}_{-1}}.

Applying our induction assumption on
\pi^{\prime}
, we have the following descent commutative diagram (the right diagram of (
6.1
)):

\textstyle{\pi^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{\ell-1,\epsilon}}
\scriptstyle{{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{\pi_{\rho^{\star\prime},\Lambda^{\star\prime}_{1},\Lambda^{\star\prime}_{-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{\rho^{\prime}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime}}\otimes\iota^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}}
\textstyle{\quad\rho^{\star\prime}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}^{\star\prime}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}^{\star\prime}}.}

According to the construction of
{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}
, we have

•

|\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{1}^{\prime})|=|\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{1}^{\star\prime})|+1
;

•

|\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime})|=|\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star\prime})|+1
;

•

{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}(\pi^{\prime})\neq 0
implies that

(6.5)

\epsilon=\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda^{\prime}_{1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1})\epsilon^{\prime}\epsilon_{\rho^{\prime}}

;

•

\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon_{\rho^{\prime}}{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho^{\prime}}\iota^{\prime}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1}^{\prime})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime})\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star\prime})\varepsilon(-1)=\epsilon_{\rho}{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho}\iota^{\prime}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1}^{\prime})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime})\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})\varepsilon(-1)
.

The last equation follows from our assumption that
\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star\prime})=\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})\neq 0
and the fact that
\pi^{\prime}
occurs in
\Theta^{\epsilon^{\prime}}_{k,k^{\epsilon^{\prime}}}(\pi)
implies
\epsilon_{\rho}=\epsilon_{\rho^{\prime}}
and
{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho}={\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho^{\prime}}
. Consider the theta correspondence from
\pi
to
\pi^{\prime}
, by Corollary
4.14
(i), we have

(6.6)

\iota^{\prime}=-{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}).

Then we have

(6.7)

\epsilon^{\prime}=-\epsilon_{\rho}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime})\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})\varepsilon(-1).

Combing (
6.5
) and (
6.7
), we have

(6.8)

\epsilon=\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda^{\prime}_{1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1})\epsilon^{\prime}\epsilon_{\rho^{\prime}}=-\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda^{\prime}_{1})\epsilon_{\rho}\epsilon_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})\varepsilon(-1).

By Proposition
4.10
and Theorem
5.11
(ii), we have

•

\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1}^{\star})=-\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})
;

•

\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})=\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})
;

•

\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1}^{\prime})=-\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})
;

•

\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime})=\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})
.

Then (
6.5
) becomes

(6.9)

\epsilon=\epsilon^{\prime}\epsilon^{\star\prime}=\epsilon_{\rho}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})\varepsilon(-1),

which completed the proof of (
6.4
). Hence, our construction of
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
on
\pi
makes sense.

Construction of
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
on
[\pi]
.

According the definition of
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
on
\pi
, the Lusztig correspondence
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
has to send
\pi^{c}
to

\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{{}^{t}\Lambda_{-1}}.

Then by Theorem
5.12
, we have

•

{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{FJ}}_{\ell,-\epsilon}(\pi)=0
;

•

{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{FJ}}_{\ell,\epsilon}(\pi^{c})=0
;

•

{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{FJ}}_{\ell,-\epsilon}(\pi^{c})=(\pi^{\star})^{c}
.

To check whether
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
is well defined, we have to check the commutativity of the following diagram

\textstyle{\pi^{c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{FJ}}_{\ell,-\epsilon}}
\scriptstyle{{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{(\pi^{\star})^{c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{{}^{t}\Lambda_{-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{-\epsilon}}
\textstyle{\quad\rho^{\star}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}^{\star}}\otimes\pi_{{}^{t}\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}},}

which can be obtained from direct calculations, and will be left to the reader.

Compatibility with theta correspondence for odd orthogonal group.

We have constructed that our choice of canonical Lusztig correspondence
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
on
\mathrm{Sp}_{2k}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Now, we are going to check the condition (2) in Theorem
6.1
.
In other words, we need to check

\iota^{\prime}={\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})\varepsilon(-1)^{\mathrm{rank}(\Lambda_{1})}.

and the commutativity of the following diagram (the top diagram of (
6.1
))

\textstyle{\pi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Theta}
\scriptstyle{{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Theta_{k,k^{\epsilon^{\prime}}}^{*}}
\textstyle{\quad\rho^{\prime}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}}\otimes\iota^{\prime}.}

We have already calculated
\iota^{\prime}
by Corollary
4.14
(i) (see (
6.6
)).
Recall that
{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho}={\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho^{\prime}}
and
\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1}^{\prime})=-\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})
. We have

\iota^{\prime}={\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}).

Since
\pi
is basic, we conclude that
\Lambda_{1}
corresponds to a unipotent cuspidal representation and
\mathrm{rank}(\Lambda_{1})
is of form
h(h+1)
. So

\iota^{\prime}={\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})\varepsilon(-1)^{\mathrm{rank}(\Lambda_{1})}.

The rest is to check

\Theta_{\epsilon^{\prime}\epsilon_{\rho}}(\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}})=\pi_{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}}.

It is equivalent to say that the theta correspondence
\Theta_{\epsilon^{\prime}\epsilon_{\rho}}(\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}})
goes down. By (
6.7
), we have

\epsilon^{\prime}\epsilon_{\rho}=-\varepsilon(-1)\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})=\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}).

According to Theorem
4.7
,
\Theta_{\epsilon^{\prime}\epsilon_{\rho}}(\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}})
goes down.

Compatibility with theta correspondence for even orthogonal group.

We have proved that our choice of canonical Lusztig correspondence
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
satisfies the condition (2) in Theorem
6.1
. Next, we are going to check the condition (3).
In order to reduce notations, we still use superscript
\prime
and
\star\prime
in this section. However, they have different meaning. Readers should not confuse them.

Let
k^{\pm}
be the first theta occurrence indexes with respect to the Witt towers
{\bf{{O}}}_{\mathrm{even}}^{\pm}
. By Proposition
4.10
, there exists
k^{\epsilon^{\prime}}
such that
k^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq k
. Denote the irreducible basic representation
\Theta^{\epsilon^{\prime}}_{k,k^{\epsilon^{\prime}}}(\pi)
by
\pi^{\prime}=\pi_{\rho^{\prime},\Lambda_{1}^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1}}
. And we have
\Theta^{\epsilon^{\prime}}_{k^{\epsilon^{\prime}},k}(\pi^{\prime})=\pi
. Similarly, let
k^{\star\pm}
be the first theta occurrence index of
\pi^{\star}
with respect to the Witt tower
{\bf{{O}}}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{\pm}
, and denote the smaller one by
k^{\star\prime}
, and denote the correspondence group by
\mathrm{O}_{2k^{\star\prime}+1}^{\epsilon^{\star\prime}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Let
\Theta^{\epsilon^{\star\prime}}_{k^{\star},k^{\star\prime}}(\pi^{\star})=\pi^{\star\prime}=\pi_{\rho^{\star\prime},\Lambda^{\star\prime}_{1},\Lambda^{\star\prime}_{-1},\iota^{\star\prime}}
.

Consider the following diagram:

(6.10)

The left side of the above diagram has been determined by previous calculation. We now are going to check that the left side is compatible with the rest of the above diagram.

Assume that
k^{\epsilon^{\prime}}=k
. We construct the canonical Lusztig correspondence
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
on
\mathrm{O}_{2k^{\epsilon^{\prime}}}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
to be the Lusztig correspondence such that the top diagram is commutative. It is easy to check that this choice of canonical Lusztig correspondence
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}
makes the right diagram commutative. Asume that
k^{\epsilon^{\prime}}<k
. Similar to the odd orthogonal group case, we first use the bottom theta diagram to get information of
\pi_{\rho^{\star\prime},\Lambda^{\star\prime}_{1},\Lambda^{\star\prime}_{-1},\iota^{\star\prime}}
. Then describe
\pi_{\rho^{\prime},\Lambda_{1}^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1}}
by the right side diagram. Finally, we check whether the top theta diagram is commutative.

From the bottom commutative diagram, as before, we know that

•

\rho^{\star\prime}=\rho^{\star}
;

•

\Lambda_{-1}^{\star\prime}=\Lambda_{1}^{\star}
;

•

\iota^{\star\prime}=\pi^{\star}(-I)\epsilon(-1)^{k^{\star}}={\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho^{\star}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda^{\star}_{-1})
.

Since the theta lifting in the bottom of diagram (
6.10
) goes down, the theta lifting
\Theta_{\epsilon^{\star\prime}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}}}(\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}})
still goes down. Then by Theorem
4.7
, we have

\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\epsilon^{\star\prime}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}}&\textrm{ if }\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})\neq\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-\\
-\end{smallmatrix}\right);\\
\epsilon^{\star\prime}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}}\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})&\textrm{ otherwise}.\\
\end{array}\right.

By our construction of
{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}
, we have

\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})=\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1}).

Since the theta lifting of
\pi
goes down, by Theorem
4.7
, we have

\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})=\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}).

Therefore

\epsilon^{\star\prime}=\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}}\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}).

Consider the two descent diagrams and
{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}
. We have

•

\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{1}^{\prime})=-{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho^{\star\prime}}\iota^{\star\prime}=-{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho^{\star\prime}}{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho^{\star}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda^{\star}_{-1})=-\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda^{\star}_{-1})=-\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})
;

•

Note that
\epsilon=\varepsilon(-1)\epsilon^{\prime}\epsilon^{\star\prime}
and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})
. By our construction of
{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}
, we know that
{\mathcal{D}}_{\ell^{\prime},\epsilon}(\pi^{\prime})\neq 0
implies that
\epsilon=\epsilon_{\rho^{\star\prime}}\epsilon_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{1})\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1})
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(-1)\epsilon^{\star\prime}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}}\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{1}^{\prime})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda^{\prime}_{1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon(-1)\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{1}^{\prime})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1})\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})=-\varepsilon(-1)\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}).

If some of
\Lambda^{\prime}_{\pm 1}
are trivial, we need not to check the corresponding part. So we assume that they are not trivial, that is,
\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda^{\prime}_{\pm 1})
are always well defined.
Now we going to check that the above data makes the following diagram commutative

\textstyle{\pi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Theta}
\scriptstyle{\cal{L}}
\textstyle{\pi_{\rho^{\prime},\Lambda_{1}^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cal{L}}
\textstyle{\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\otimes\Theta\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\quad\rho^{\prime}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime}},}

or equivalently checking

•

\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{1}^{\prime})=-\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})
(Since
\pi
goes down in the above theta lifting);

•

\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime})=\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1})
.

The first equation has already been proved. For the second one, by our construction of
{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}
, we have

\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})=-\varepsilon(-1)\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}).

Hence

\displaystyle\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime})=

\displaystyle-\varepsilon(-1)\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varepsilon(-1)\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})(-\varepsilon(-1)\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}))=\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}).

Corollary 6.3
.

Each facet of the diagram (
6.1
) is commutative.

7.
A finite field instance of relative Langlands duality

7.1.
Calculation of Gan-Gross-Prasad problem

Using the Lusztig correspondence described in Theorem
6.1
, we can refine the conditions that are strongly relevant for a pair of representations. This refinement allows us to rephrase the main theorems in
[
Wang1
]
and determine the multiplicity in the finite Gan-Gross-Prasad problem in a combinatorial manner.

Theorem 7.1
.

(1)

Let
\pi=\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{bas}}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n})
and
\pi^{\star}=\pi_{\rho^{\star},\Lambda^{\star}_{1},\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{bas}}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n^{\star}})
. Then
(\pi,\pi^{\star})
is
\epsilon
-strongly relevant if and only if there are
{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\in\{\pm\}
such that

•

\epsilon=\epsilon_{\rho}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}
;

•

{\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{1}^{\star})=-{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}){\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{-1})+\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})
,

•

{\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})=-{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})\varepsilon(-1){\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{1})-{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})\varepsilon(-1)
.

(2)

Let
\pi=\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{bas}}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n})
and
\pi^{\star}=\pi_{\rho^{\star},\Lambda^{\star}_{1},\Lambda^{\star}_{-1},\iota^{\star}}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{bas}}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon^{\star}}_{2n^{\star}+1})
. Then
(\pi,\pi^{\star})
is strongly relevant if and only if there are
{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\in\{\pm\}
such that

•

\epsilon^{\star}=\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})\varepsilon(-1)
;

•

\iota^{\star}=-{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho^{\star}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}
;

•

{\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{1}^{\star})=-{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1}){\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{1})+\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})
,

•

{\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})=-{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}){\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{-1})+\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})
.

Proof.

We only prove (i). The proof of (ii) is similar.

According to Corollary
5.7
, we have

(7.1)

|\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{1}^{\star})|=|\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{-1})|\pm 1\textrm{ and }|\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{1})|=|\mathrm{def}(\Lambda^{\star}_{-1})|\pm 1.

Pick
\epsilon^{\prime}\in\{\pm\}
such that
\pi
goes down with respect to the Witt tower
{\bf O}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}_{\mathrm{even}}
, and consider its first theta lifting
\Theta(\pi)
. By Theorem
4.7
and Theorem
6.1
(iii), we have

•

\Theta(\pi)=\pi_{\rho^{\prime},\Lambda_{1}^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1}}

•

\rho^{\prime}=\rho
;

•

\Theta(\pi_{\Lambda_{1}})=\pi_{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}}
is the unipotent basic representation of
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}_{2n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
with
\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{1}^{\prime})=-\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{1})+\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})
,
\epsilon^{\prime}=\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1}^{\prime})
, and
n^{\prime}=\left(\frac{\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{1}^{\prime})}{2}\right)^{2}
;

•

\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1}=\Lambda_{-1}
.

Hence

(7.2)

\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1}^{\prime})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1})=\epsilon_{\rho}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}),

and

{\bf n}_{\mathrm{even}}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\pi)={\bf n}_{\mathrm{even}}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\pi_{\Lambda_{1}})=\frac{|\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{1})-1|}{2}.

Consider the first theta lifting
\Theta(\pi^{\star})
of
\pi^{\star}
with respect to
{\bf O}^{\varepsilon(-1)\cdot\epsilon\epsilon^{\prime}}_{\mathrm{odd}}
. With the same theta argument, we have

{\bf n}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{\varepsilon(-1)\cdot\epsilon\epsilon^{\prime}}(\pi^{\star})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\textrm{ if }\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})=0;\\
\frac{|\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})|}{2}&\textrm{ if }\epsilon=\epsilon^{\prime}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}}\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})\varepsilon(-1);\\
\frac{-|\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})|}{2}&\textrm{ if }\epsilon=-\epsilon^{\prime}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}}\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})\varepsilon(-1).\end{array}\right.

To make sure
(\pi,\pi^{\star})

\epsilon
-strongly relevant, firstly,
(\pi,\pi^{\star})
has to be
\epsilon
-relevant. In other words, either
{\bf n}_{\mathrm{even}}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\pi)={\bf n}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{\varepsilon(-1)\cdot\epsilon\epsilon^{\prime}}(\pi^{\star})-1
or
{\bf n}_{\mathrm{even}}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\pi)={\bf n}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{\varepsilon(-1)\cdot\epsilon\epsilon^{\prime}}(\pi^{\star})
.
Hence
{\bf n}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{\varepsilon(-1)\cdot\epsilon\epsilon^{\prime}}(\pi^{\star})
can not be negative. We will only prove the case
{\bf n}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{\varepsilon(-1)\cdot\epsilon\epsilon^{\prime}}(\pi^{\star})>0
. The proof for the case
{\bf n}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{\varepsilon(-1)\cdot\epsilon\epsilon^{\prime}}(\pi^{\star})=0
is similar. In the positive case, we have
\epsilon=\epsilon^{\prime}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}}\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})\varepsilon(-1)
. Using (
7.2
),

(7.3)

\epsilon=\epsilon_{\rho}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})\varepsilon(-1).

On the other hand, the pair
(\pi^{\star},\pi)
have to be
\varepsilon(-1)\epsilon
-relevant. With the similar argument, we have

(7.4)

\epsilon=\epsilon_{\rho}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}}\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1}^{\star})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}).

Combining (
7.3
) and (
7.4
), we have

(7.5)

\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})=\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1}^{\star})\varepsilon(-1).

Set

(7.6)

{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}=-\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}),\textrm{ and }{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}=-\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1}^{\star})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda_{-1}).

Substitute (
7.5
) and (
7.6
) into (
7.3
), we have

\epsilon=\epsilon_{\rho}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}.

The defect of
\Lambda_{1}^{\star}
and
\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}
is obtained by (
7.1
), (
7.5
), and (
7.6
).
∎

Theorem
7.1
allows us to rewrite
[
Wang1
, Theorem 1.4]
as follows.

Theorem 7.2
.

(1)

Let
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n})
and
\pi_{\rho^{\star},\Lambda^{\star}_{1},\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n^{\star}})
, where
n\geqslant n^{\star}
. Assume that
\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon_{-1}}_{2m})
,

\rho=\prod\pi[a]

and

\rho^{\star}=\prod\pi^{\star}[a],

where
\pi[a]
(resp.
\pi^{\star}[a]
) is the irreducible unipotent representation corresponding to the partition
\lambda[a]
(resp.
\lambda^{\star}[a]
).

m_{\epsilon}(\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}},\pi_{\rho^{\star},\Lambda^{\star}_{1},\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}})\neq 0

if and only if
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}}
and
\pi_{\rho^{\star},\Lambda^{\star}_{1},\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}
satisfy the following conditions:

(i)

there are
{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\in\{\pm\}
such that

\displaystyle\epsilon={{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\epsilon_{\rho}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}},

\displaystyle\Upsilon(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})^{-{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}{\epsilon_{-1}\varepsilon(-1)}}\preccurlyeq\Upsilon(\Lambda_{1})^{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}},\quad\Upsilon(\Lambda_{1})^{-{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}}\preccurlyeq\Upsilon(\Lambda^{\star}_{-1})^{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}{\epsilon_{-1}}\varepsilon(-1)},

\displaystyle\Upsilon(\Lambda_{1}^{\star})^{\epsilon_{-1}}\preccurlyeq\Upsilon(\Lambda_{-1})^{-{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}},\quad\Upsilon(\Lambda_{-1})^{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}}\preccurlyeq\Upsilon(\Lambda^{\star}_{1})^{-\epsilon_{-1}},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{1}^{\star})=-\epsilon_{-1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}{\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{-1})+\epsilon_{-1},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})=-\epsilon_{-1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\varepsilon(-1){\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{1})-{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\epsilon_{-1}\varepsilon(-1).

(ii)

\begin{cases}\textrm{$\lambda[a]$ and $\lambda^{\prime}[-a]$ are 2-transverse},&\textrm{ if $\#[a]$ is odd};\\
\textrm{$\lambda[a]$ and $\lambda^{\prime}[-a]$ are close},&\textrm{ if $\#[a]$ is even}.\end{cases}

(2)

Let
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(\mathrm{O}_{2n}^{\epsilon})
and
\pi_{\rho^{\star},\Lambda^{\star}_{1},\Lambda^{\star}_{-1},\iota^{\star}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon^{\star}}_{2n^{\star}+1})
, where
n>n^{\star}
. Assume that
\pi_{\Lambda_{\pm 1}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon_{\pm}}_{2m_{\pm}})
,

\rho=\prod\pi[a]

and

\rho^{\star}=\prod\pi^{\star}[a],

where
\pi[a]
(resp.
\pi^{\star}[a]
) is the irreducible unipotent representation corresponding to the partition
\lambda[a]
(resp.
\lambda^{\star}[a]
).

m_{\epsilon}(\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}},\pi_{\rho^{\star},\Lambda^{\star}_{1},\Lambda^{\star}_{-1},\iota^{\star}})\neq 0

if and only if
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}}
and
\pi_{\rho^{\star},\Lambda^{\star}_{1},\Lambda^{\star}_{-1},\iota^{\star}}
satisfy the following conditions:

(i)

there are
{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\in\{\pm\}
such that

\displaystyle\epsilon={{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\epsilon_{\rho}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}},

\displaystyle\Upsilon(\Lambda_{1}^{\star})^{\epsilon_{+}}\preccurlyeq\Upsilon(\Lambda_{1})^{-{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}},\quad\Upsilon(\Lambda_{1})^{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}}\preccurlyeq\Upsilon(\Lambda^{\star}_{1})^{-\epsilon_{+}},

\displaystyle\Upsilon(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})^{\epsilon_{-}}\preccurlyeq\Upsilon(\Lambda_{-1})^{-{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}},\quad\Upsilon(\Lambda_{-1})^{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}}\preccurlyeq\Upsilon(\Lambda^{\star}_{-1})^{-\epsilon_{-}},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{1}^{\star})=-\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1}){{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{1})+\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1});

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})=-\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}){{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}{\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{-1})+\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1});

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\star}=\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1}){{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\varepsilon(-1);

\displaystyle\iota^{\star}=-{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho^{\star}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}\varepsilon(-1)^{\mathrm{rank}(\Lambda^{\star}_{-1})}.

(ii)

\begin{cases}\textrm{$\lambda[a]$ and $\lambda^{\prime}[a]$ are 2-transverse},&\textrm{ if $\#[a]$ is odd};\\
\textrm{$\lambda[a]$ and $\lambda^{\prime}[a]$ are close},&\textrm{ if $\#[a]$ is even}.\end{cases}

(3)

Let
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},\iota}\in{\mathcal{E}}(\mathrm{O}_{2n+1}^{\epsilon})
and
\pi_{\rho^{\star},\Lambda^{\star}_{1},\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon^{\star}}_{2n^{\star}+1})
, where
n\geqslant n^{\star}
. Assume that
\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{\pm 1}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon^{\star}_{\pm}}_{2m^{\star}_{\pm}})
,

\rho=\prod\pi[a]

and

\rho^{\star}=\prod\pi^{\star}[a],

where
\pi[a]
(resp.
\pi^{\star}[a]
) is the irreducible unipotent representation corresponding to the partition
\lambda[a]
(resp.
\lambda^{\star}[a]
). Then

m_{\epsilon}(\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},\iota},\pi_{\rho^{\star},\Lambda^{\star}_{1},\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}})\neq 0

if and only if
\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},\iota}
and
\pi_{\rho^{\star},\Lambda^{\star}_{1},\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}
satisfy the following conditions:

(i)

there are
{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\in\{\pm\}
such that

\displaystyle\epsilon=\epsilon{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}},

\displaystyle\Upsilon(\Lambda_{1}^{\star})^{-\iota^{\prime}}\preccurlyeq\Upsilon(\Lambda_{1})^{-{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}},\quad\Upsilon(\Lambda_{1})^{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}}\preccurlyeq\Upsilon(\Lambda^{\star}_{1})^{\iota^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\Upsilon(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})^{-\iota^{\prime}\epsilon\epsilon_{\rho}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\varepsilon(-1)}\preccurlyeq\Upsilon(\Lambda_{-1})^{-{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}},\quad\Upsilon(\Lambda_{-1})^{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}}\preccurlyeq\Upsilon(\Lambda^{\star}_{-1})^{\iota^{\prime}\epsilon\epsilon_{\rho}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\varepsilon(-1)},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{1}^{\star})=\iota^{\prime}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}\mathrm{def}(\Lambda_{1})-\iota^{\prime};

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})=\iota^{\prime}\epsilon\epsilon_{\rho}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}\varepsilon(-1){\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{-1})-\iota^{\prime}\epsilon\epsilon_{\rho}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\varepsilon(-1);

where
\iota^{\prime}=\iota{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho}\varepsilon(-1)^{\mathrm{rank}(\Lambda_{-1})}
.

(ii)

\begin{cases}\textrm{$\lambda[a]$ and $\lambda^{\prime}[a]$ are 2-transverse},&\textrm{ if $\#[a]$ is odd};\\
\textrm{$\lambda[a]$ and $\lambda^{\prime}[a]$ are close},&\textrm{ if $\#[a]$ is even}.\end{cases}

7.2.
The duality between Gan-Gross-Prasad problem and Theta correspondence

The Gan-Gross-Prasad problem is naturally related to the theta correspondence in the context of relative Langlands duality of Ben-Zvi-Sakellaridis-Venkatesh
[
BZSV
]
in the local field case. In the finite field case, a similar duality for unipotent representations of unitary groups has been proven in
[
LW2
]
. In
[
Wang1
]
, we have established the duality between the Gan-Gross-Prasad problem for special orthogonal groups and the theta correspondence for the symplectic-even orthogonal dual pair.
Based on the above description of the Gan-Gross-Prasad problem, we can extend the aforementioned duality to full orthogonal groups and symplectic groups.

Theorem 7.3
.

(1)

Let
\pi=\pi_{-,\Lambda_{1},-}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},1)
, and
\pi^{\star}=\pi_{-,-,\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n^{\star}},s)
where
s=(-I,1)
with
I
being the identity in
\mathrm{O}_{2n}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\in\mathrm{SO}_{2n+1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{*}
. Let
\pi^{\prime}:={\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}(\pi^{\star})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}_{2n^{\star}},1)
.
Then

m_{\epsilon}(\pi,\pi^{\star})=\langle\cal{AC}(\pi)\otimes\cal{AC}(-\varepsilon(-1)\epsilon{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\pi^{\prime}),\omega_{n,n^{\star}}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\rangle,

where
\cal{AC}
is the Alvis-Curtis duality, and
\omega_{n,n^{\star}}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}
is the Weil representation for
(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}_{2n^{\star}})
and

-\varepsilon(-1)\epsilon{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\pi^{\prime}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\pi^{\prime}&\textrm{ if }-\varepsilon(-1)\epsilon{\epsilon^{\prime}}=+;\\
\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi^{\prime}&\textrm{ if }-\varepsilon(-1)\epsilon{\epsilon^{\prime}}=-.\end{array}\right.

In other words, the above equation can be visualized as a diagram:

\textstyle{\pi\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
GGP
\scriptstyle{\cal{AC}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\pi^{\star},\ \pi^{\star}\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n^{\star}},s)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cal{AC}\circ(-\varepsilon(-1)\epsilon{\epsilon^{\prime}})\circ{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{\pi\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
theta lfiting
\textstyle{\bigoplus\pi^{\prime},\ \pi^{\prime}\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}_{2n^{\star}},1).}

(2)

Let
\pi=\pi_{-,\Lambda_{1},-}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n},1)
, and
\pi^{\star}=\pi_{-,\Lambda_{1}^{\star},-,\iota^{\star}}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon^{\star}}_{2n^{\star}+1},1)
. Let
\pi^{\prime}:={\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}(\pi^{\star})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n^{\star}},1)
.
Then

m_{\varepsilon(-1)\epsilon\epsilon^{\star}}(\pi,\pi^{\star})=\langle\cal{AC}(\pi^{\prime})\otimes\cal{AC}(-\iota^{\star}\epsilon\cdot\pi),\omega_{n,n^{\star}}^{\epsilon}\rangle,

The above equation can be visualized as a diagram:

\textstyle{\pi\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n},1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
GGP
\scriptstyle{\cal{AC}\circ(-\iota^{\star}\epsilon)}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\pi^{\star},\ \pi^{\star}\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon^{\star}}_{2n^{\star}+1},1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cal{AC}\circ{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{\pi\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n},1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
theta lfiting
\textstyle{\bigoplus\pi^{\prime},\ \pi^{\prime}\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n^{\star}},1).}

(3)

Let
\pi=\pi_{-,\Lambda_{1},-,\iota}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n+1},1)
, and
\pi^{\star}=\pi_{-,\Lambda_{1}^{\star},-}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon^{\star}}_{2n^{\star}},1)
. Let
\pi^{\prime}:={\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}(\pi)\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},1)
.
Then

m_{\epsilon\epsilon^{\star}}(\pi,\pi^{\star})=\langle\cal{AC}(\pi^{\prime})\otimes\cal{AC}(-\iota\epsilon^{\star}\cdot\pi),\omega_{n,n^{\star}}^{\epsilon}\rangle,

The above equation can be visualized as a diagram:

\textstyle{\pi\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n+1},1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
GGP
\scriptstyle{\cal{AC}\circ{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\pi^{\star},\ \pi^{\star}\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon^{\star}}_{2n^{\star}},1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cal{AC}\circ(-\iota^{\star}\epsilon)}
\textstyle{\pi\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n},1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
theta lfiting
\textstyle{\bigoplus\pi^{\prime},\ \pi^{\prime}\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon^{\star}}_{2n^{\star}},1).}

Proof.

We only prove (1). In this case, the conditions in Theorem
7.2
means that
there are
{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\in\{\pm\}
such that

\displaystyle\epsilon={{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}},

\displaystyle\Upsilon(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})^{-{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}{\varepsilon(-1)}}\preccurlyeq\Upsilon(\Lambda_{1})^{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}},\quad\Upsilon(\Lambda_{1})^{-{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}}\preccurlyeq\Upsilon(\Lambda^{\star}_{-1})^{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}{}\varepsilon(-1)},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{-1}^{\star})=-{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\varepsilon(-1)({\mathrm{def}}(\Lambda_{1})-{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}).

The last equation implies that
{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}=\epsilon^{\star}
. Then (1) immediately follows from Theorem
4.3
.
∎

The general case is much more complicated because many signs are involved. However, once we ignore them, there exists a duality between theta lifting and Gan-Gross-Prasad problem.

Theorem 7.4
.

(1)

Let
\pi\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n})
, and
\pi^{\star}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n^{\star}})
.
Then we have the following commutative diagram up to a twist of the sgn character on orthogonal groups:

\textstyle{\pi\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\cal{AC}\circ{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\pi^{\star},\ \pi^{\star}\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{Sp}_{2n^{\star}},s)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cal{AC}\circ{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
theta lfiting
\textstyle{\bigoplus\pi^{\star},\ \rho^{\star}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}}

where the sum in the bottom right corner runs over representations such that

•

\rho=\prod_{[a],a\neq\pm 1}\pi[a]
and
\rho^{\star}=\prod_{[a],a\neq\pm 1}\pi^{\star}[a]

•

For each
[a]
,
\pi^{\star}[-a]
appears in theta lifting of
\pi[a]
;

•

{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}(\pi^{\star}_{\Lambda_{1}})
appears in theta lifting of
\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}
;

•

\pi^{\star}_{\Lambda_{-1}}
appears in theta lifting of
{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}(\pi_{\Lambda_{1}})
.

(2)

Let
\pi\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n})
, and
\pi^{\star}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon^{\star}}_{2n^{\star}+1})
.
Then we have the following commutative diagram up to a twist of the sgn character on orthogonal groups:

\textstyle{\pi\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
GGP
\scriptstyle{\cal{AC}\circ{\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\pi^{\star},\ \pi^{\star}\in\cal{E}(\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon^{\star}}_{2n^{\star}},s)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

theta lfiting
\textstyle{\bigoplus\pi^{\star},\ \rho^{\star}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}\otimes\iota^{\star}}

where the sum in the bottom right corner runs over representations such that

•

\rho=\prod_{[a],a\neq\pm 1}\pi[a]
and

,

appears in theta lifting of
\pi[a]
;

•

appears in theta lifting of
\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}
;

appears in theta lifting of

.

7.3.
A description of the first descent case

Although the description of the multiplicity in the finite Gan-Gross-Prasad problem (as shown in Theorem
7.2
) is quite complicated, the first descent case of the problem is simple. This case is similar to the first occurrence case of theta lifting. We have already constructed a functor
{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}
for basic representations, and we can read the action of
{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}
for all representations by referring to Theorem
7.2
. To calculate the first descent of an irreducible representation
\pi
, we need to find the representations that live in the smallest groups, and satisfy the conditions in Theorem
7.2
. This process is a direct but complex combinatorial calculation, which we will leave to the readers. Here, we present the results.

Let
\Lambda=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a_{1},\cdots,a_{m}\\
b_{1},\cdots,b_{m^{\prime}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
be a symbol. Set

\zeta(\Lambda)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{+\}&\textrm{ if }a_{1}>b_{1}\\
\{-\}&\textrm{ if }a_{1}<b_{1}\\
\{\pm\}&\textrm{ if }a_{1}=b_{1}\\
\end{array}\right.

Assume that
G_{n}=\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}
, and
\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(C_{G_{n}^{*}}(s))
. Then we set

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}(\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}})=\begin{cases}\bigoplus\rho^{\star}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}&\textrm{ if }\epsilon={{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\epsilon_{\rho}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}};\\
0&\textrm{ otherwise, }\end{cases}

where the direct sum runs over irreducible representations
\rho^{\star}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}
such that

•

\rho^{\star}=\rho^{-}_{1}
;

•

-{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}\in\zeta({}^{{\bf\mathrm{d}}}\Lambda_{1})
, and
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\in\zeta({}^{{\bf\mathrm{d}}}\Lambda_{-1})
;

•

The symbol
\Lambda_{1}^{\star}
is obtained by following these steps: (1) Take the Alvis-Curtis dual
{}^{\bf\mathrm{d}}\Lambda_{-1}
, (2) Removing the maximal number in the top (resp. bottom) line in
{}^{\bf\mathrm{d}}\Lambda_{-1}
if
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}=+
(resp.
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}=-
), (3) Swapping the arrays of
\Lambda_{-1}
if
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})=+
, (4) Retake the Alvis-Curtis dual;

•

The symbol
\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}
is obtained by following these steps: (1) Take the Alvis-Curtis dual
{}^{\bf\mathrm{d}}\Lambda_{1}
, (2) Removing the maximal number in the top (resp. bottom) line in
{}^{\bf\mathrm{d}}\Lambda_{1}
if
-{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}=+
(resp.
-{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}=-
), (3) Swapping the arrays of
\Lambda_{1}
if
\varepsilon(-1){{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})=+
, (4) Retake the Alvis-Curtis dual.

Assume that
G_{n}=\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n}
, and
\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(C_{G_{n}^{*}}(s))
. Then we set

{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}})=\begin{cases}\bigoplus\rho^{\star}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}\otimes\iota^{\star}&\textrm{ if }\epsilon^{\prime}={{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\epsilon_{\rho}\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}};\\
0&\textrm{ otherwise, }\end{cases}

where the direct sum runs over irreducible representations
\rho^{\star}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}\otimes\iota^{\star}
such that

•

\rho^{\star}=\rho_{1}
;

•

{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}\in\zeta({}^{{\bf\mathrm{d}}}\Lambda_{1})
, and
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\in\zeta({}^{{\bf\mathrm{d}}}\Lambda_{-1})
;

•

The symbol
\Lambda_{1}^{\star}
is obtained by following these steps: (1) Take the Alvis-Curtis dual
{}^{\bf\mathrm{d}}\Lambda_{1}
, (2) Removing the maximal number in the top (resp. bottom) line in
{}^{\bf\mathrm{d}}\Lambda_{1}
if
{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}=+
(resp.
{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}=-
), (3) Swapping the arrays of
\Lambda_{1}
if
{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})=+
(4) Retake the Alvis-Curtis dual;

•

The symbol
\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}
is obtained by following these steps: (1) Take the Alvis-Curtis dual
{}^{\bf\mathrm{d}}\Lambda_{-1}
, (2) Removing the maximal number in the top (resp. bottom) line in
{}^{\bf\mathrm{d}}\Lambda_{-1}
if
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}=+
(resp.
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}=-
), (3) Swapping the arrays of
\Lambda_{-1}
if
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})=+
(4) Retake the Alvis-Curtis dual;

•

\iota^{\star}=-{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho^{\star}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}\varepsilon(-1)^{\mathrm{rank}(\Lambda_{-1})}
.

Assume that
G_{n}=\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n+1}
, and
\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}}\otimes\iota\in{\mathcal{E}}(C_{G_{n}^{*}}(s))
. Then we set

{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(\rho\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\iota)=\begin{cases}\bigoplus\rho^{\star}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}&\textrm{ if }\epsilon^{\prime}={{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\epsilon\epsilon_{\rho^{\star}};\\
0&\textrm{ otherwise, }\end{cases}

where the direct sum runs over irreducible representations
\rho^{\star}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\Lambda^{\star}_{-1}}
such that

•

\rho^{\star}=\rho_{1}
;

•

{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}\in\zeta({}^{{\bf\mathrm{d}}}\Lambda_{1})
, and
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}\in\zeta({}^{{\bf\mathrm{d}}}\Lambda_{-1})
;

•

let
\iota^{\prime}=\iota{\widetilde{\iota}}_{\rho}\varepsilon(-1)^{\mathrm{rank}(\Lambda_{-1})}
;

•

The symbol
\Lambda_{1}^{\star}
is obtained by following these steps:
(1) Take the Alvis-Curtis dual
{}^{\bf\mathrm{d}}\Lambda_{1}
, (2) Removing the maximal number in the top (resp. bottom) line in
{}^{\bf\mathrm{d}}\Lambda_{1}
if
{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}=+
(resp.
{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}=-
), (3) Swapping the arrays of
\Lambda_{1}
if
{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}\iota^{\prime}=-
(4) Retake the Alvis-Curtis dual;

•

The symbol
\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}
iis obtained by following these steps:
(1) Take the Alvis-Curtis dual
{}^{\bf\mathrm{d}}\Lambda_{-1}
, (2) Removing the maximal number in the top (resp. bottom) line in
{}^{\bf\mathrm{d}}\Lambda_{1}
if
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}=+
(resp.
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}=-
), (3) Swapping the arrays of
\Lambda_{-1}
if
\iota^{\prime}\epsilon\epsilon_{\rho}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}\varepsilon(-1)=-
, (4) Retake the Alvis-Curtis dual.

For basic representations, the definition of
{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}
coincides with
{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}
defined in Section
7
.

In most cases, the first descent of an irreducible representation is also irreducible. However, there is a non-irreducible first descent case that occurs only in a specific situation. In the
{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}
functor, we need to remove the largest element of some symbols. In most cases, the largest element is unique, which is the irreducible first descent case. However, when the largest elements are not unique, we need to choose one of them. Each choice will then give us an irreducible constituent of the first descent.

Example 7.5
.

Consider the trivial representation
\bf{1}
of
\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, which corresponds to the symbol

\Lambda_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-\end{pmatrix},\quad\textrm{with }{}^{{\bf\mathrm{d}}}\Lambda_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}1,0\\
1\end{pmatrix}.

In this case,
{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}
can be both
+
and
-
. Since the representation
{\bf 1}
does not have the
(-1)
-part and the empty
(-1)
-part corresponds to the trivial symbol

\begin{pmatrix}-\\
-\end{pmatrix},

the sign
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}}
can be both
+
and
-
. Then we have the following descent diagram

\textstyle{{\bf{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime}}\oplus\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime t}}\oplus\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}^{\star t}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bf{1}\oplus\bf{1}}
\textstyle{\bf{1}\oplus\bf{1}}

where

\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}\ \textrm{ and }\ \Lambda_{-1}^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}1,0\\
-\end{pmatrix}.

The representations
\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime}}
and
\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}}
in the descent of
\bf{1}
correspond to the pairs
({{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}})=(-,+)
and
({{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\epsilon_{\beta}}}})=(+,-)
, respectively. Note that
\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime}}
and
\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}}
have different cuspidal supports. Namely,
\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}^{\star}}
itself is cuspidal, while the cuspidal support of
\pi_{\Lambda_{-1}^{\prime}}
is the trivial representation of
\mathrm{Sp}_{0}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
.
Thus in general different choices of
{{\color[rgb]{1,0.75,0.75}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}}}
in the descent may give rise to representations with different cuspidal supports.

Combining Theorem
6.1
and Theorem
7.2
, we find that the diagram (
6.1
) is not only commutative for basic representations, but also for all representations.

Corollary 7.6
.

Let
\pi\in\mathcal{E}(G)
where
G
is either a symplectic group, odd orthogonal group or a even orthogonal group. Then we have the following commutative diagram

Corollary 7.7
.

Let
G
be a symplectic group or orthogonal group. Then we have

\begin{array}[]{c|cccc}G&\pi&\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi&\pi^{c}&\chi\cdot\pi\\
\hline\cr\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}&\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}}&&\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}^{t}}&\\
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n}&\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}}&\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1}^{t},\Lambda_{-1}^{t}}&\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1}^{t}}&\pi_{\rho^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}_{-1},\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}}\\
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n+1}&\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},\iota}&\pi_{\rho,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{-1},-\iota}&&\pi_{\rho^{\prime},\Lambda_{-1},\Lambda_{1},\iota^{\prime}\iota}\\
\end{array}

where we set

\Lambda_{\pm 1}^{\prime}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Lambda_{\pm 1}&\textrm{ if }\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})=+;\\
\Lambda_{\pm 1}^{t}&\textrm{ if }\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{1})\mathrm{pd}(\Lambda_{-1})=-,\\
\end{array}\right.

and
\iota^{\prime}=\chi_{G}(-I)
.

Proof.

We will start by providing the first three columns of the table. The symplectic and odd orthogonal cases have already been proven in Corollary
3.5
.

Next, we need to calculate
\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi
of
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. We claim that there exists an irreducible representation
\pi^{\prime}
of an odd orthogonal group such that the first descent
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{\ell,\epsilon^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime})=\pi
. In fact, by Corollary
7.6
, we know that
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{\ell,\epsilon}(\pi^{\prime})=\pi
if and only if
{\mathcal{D}}_{\epsilon}^{*}({\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}(\pi^{\prime}))={\cal{L}^{\mathrm{can}}}(\pi)
. Recall that
{\mathcal{D}}_{\epsilon}^{*}
is defined as taking the Alvis-Curtis dual of certain symbols and removing certain elements. To find
\pi^{\prime}
, we can do the opposite, which is to add some elements. By doing this, we can find an irreducible representation
\pi^{\prime}
.
Using Theorem
5.14
(iii), we have
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{\ell,\epsilon^{\prime}}(\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi^{\prime})=\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi
. Although Theorem
5.14
(iii) is only for basic representation, it is not difficult to generalize the same statement to all representations. Thus, the description of
\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi
follows from the description of
\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\pi^{\prime}
and the definition of
{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{\epsilon}
.

Let us now consider
\pi^{c}
of
\mathrm{O}^{\epsilon}_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Let
\pi^{\prime\prime}
be an irreducible representation of a symplectic group such that
\pi^{\prime\prime}
appears in the theta lifting of
\pi
. Then by
[
P3
, Lemma 5.3]
, we know that
(\pi^{\prime\prime})^{c}
appears in the theta lifting of
\pi^{c}
. Hence the description of
\pi^{c}
follows from the description of
(\pi^{\prime\prime})^{c}
and Theorem
6.1
(3).

We now turn to proving the fourth column of the table. Let
G
be an odd or even orthogonal group. Consider the first descent
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{\ell,\epsilon^{\star}}(\pi)=\pi^{\star}
of
\pi
, where
\pi^{\star}
is a representation of
G^{\star}
(a smaller orthogonal group than
G
), which may not be irreducible. By Theorem
5.13
(ii) and Theorem
5.14
(iv) (which can be generalized to all representations), we have
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{\ell,\epsilon^{\star}}(\chi_{G}\cdot\pi)=\chi_{G^{\star}}\cdot\pi^{\star}
. Then, we can obtain the description of
\chi_{G}\cdot\pi
by induction on
n
.
∎

Remark 7.8
.

The above description of representations of even orthogonal group is compatible with the fact

(\chi_{G}\cdot\pi^{c})^{c}=\mathrm{sgn}\cdot\chi_{G}\cdot\pi.
